It MUST answer the union over those members of `Hash[B | M, V]`. `V` is the member's value type. `B` is the block's return type; with no block it is the member's key type, and it is `Dynamic[top]` when the call carries a block the block pass left untyped. `&nil` passes no block. `M` is the mapping's value type when the call passes exactly one argument, positionally or as keyword arguments (`transform_keys(a: :z)`): a `HashShape` mapping's values, a `Hash[_, M]` nominal's second argument, the same read through a `Difference`, and the union of these over a union's members. A mapping whose value type cannot be read MUST contribute `Dynamic[top]` rather than nothing: an untyped argument, a raw `Hash`, a non-`Hash` nominal (a subclass, or an object `to_hash` converts), `nil`, and an open shape's unlisted entries. So MUST an empty closed shape, whose type reads narrower than its runtime value when it is a mapping filled through an alias (`m = {}; m.tap { |x| x[:a] = :z }`), since the blockless form's former `Dynamic[top]` arm no longer covers it. A literal with a `**splat` entry needs no arm of its own, however it reaches the call: its type joins the splatted hash's pairs and carries its own `Dynamic[top]` arm, which also keeps a copy the code writes into growing (the `ExpressionTyper` hash-literal rule of the Slice 5 phase 1 shape-dispatch contract below). This rule does not close the engine-wide aliasing gap: a non-empty shape filled through an alias still reads narrower than it is. An argument count the call does not fix — a `*splat`, a forwarded `...`, or keyword arguments made only of `**splat` entries, each of which may pass nothing — MUST take `M = Dynamic[top]` when the call has a block and MUST decline when it has none, because the no-argument, no-block call answers an `Enumerator`. An open receiver shape MUST add a `Dynamic[top]` arm to `K` and `V`. The tier MUST decline for `transform_keys!`, which returns `self`, for a `Hash` subclass receiver (`ActiveSupport::HashWithIndifferentAccess` overrides the method), for any other argument count, and for a union with any other member. The mapping form's block parameter MUST likewise bind to the receiver's key type ahead of the RBS probe (`IteratorDispatch` routes it to `HashTransformKeysFolding.block_param_types`), because the RBS probe does not bind it for a union of receiver shapes. The forms the tier accepts therefore get one answer on either RBS line. A declined form keeps its line's RBS answer. The rule MUST NOT generalise to reading a container argument in the generic solve: that would reopen the container walk the argument-position rule forbids. The generic solve's own reading of an argument alongside the block (the shared value class above) reads only an argument whose parameter is the bare variable, so it leaves `transform_keys`'s `K2` `Dynamic[top]`.

`RbsDispatch` reads the resolved overload's declared return type and MUST NOT reinterpret it, with two bounded exceptions. One is `Enumerable#sum`'s class-level reading, stated with the argument-position rule above. The other is issue #661. `Kernel#<=>: (untyped other) -> 0?` is the identity comparison — `0` when the operands are the same object, `nil` otherwise — and every class inherits it. When `<=>` resolves through inheritance to that declaration on an INSTANCE receiver whose class is not `Kernel`, `Object` or `BasicObject`, the tier MUST answer `Integer?` instead: the literal `0` is a value-precise claim about an operator the receiver's signature was never required to mention, so under [ADR-5](../adr/5-robustness-principle.md) it is not a statement about the call. The cost of not widening is not confined to the expression — narrowing `n = a <=> b; … if n` reads the truthy arm as the literal `0` and folds a comparison branch to `bot`. The three owning classes MUST keep `0?`, a class that DECLARES its own `<=>` MUST keep its own return type unchanged, and the exception MUST NOT generalise to other inherited `Object` / `Kernel` selectors — `to_s`, `hash` and `inspect` carry no value-precise return to lose, and what an inherited universal signature may assert about a subclass in general is a larger question this does not settle.

The nominal-acceptance contract (`Rigor::Inference::Acceptance`, the `Nominal` self / `Constant` other route) MUST satisfy:

- A `Nominal[C, [T…]]` self MUST decide a `Constant[v]` other on the value's class alone (`v.is_a?(klass(C))`, or the value's ancestor chain when `C` is not loadable), ignoring its own `type_args`. A value-pinned literal carries a value, not a generic instantiation, so there is nothing in it to refute the args against and refuting them anyway would reject correct code.
- **A `Range` value is the one exception** (issue #833). The engine builds `Constant[Range]` only when both endpoints are static, so the element the range yields IS known. When the value is a `Range` and the self carries EXACTLY one type arg `T`, acceptance MUST additionally require that every non-`nil` endpoint, lifted to `Constant[endpoint]`, is accepted by `T` under the same mode; a `nil` endpoint MUST contribute nothing, because a beginless or endless literal inhabits `Range[T]` for every `T` (`Range[Integer?]` is core RBS's spelling of the slicing parameter). A raw `Range` with no type args, any other type-arg arity, a range with no endpoint at all (`nil..nil`), and a range whose endpoints are not literals MUST all keep the class-level answer. The rule MUST NOT be extended to any other constant class. Without it `Nominal[Range, [Integer]]` accepted `0.0...1.0` and overload selection pinned whichever `Range[…]` arm came first in declaration order — for `Kernel#rand` that is `(::Range[Integer]) -> Integer?`, ahead of every Float arm.
- **Known coarse spot, deliberately not special-cased.** A mixed-endpoint literal (`1.0..2`) is rejected by `Range[Integer]` and by `Range[Float]` alike, so overload selection falls through the strict pass to the gradual pass and then to the first-overload fallback, exactly as it did before the exception existed. Reading such a literal precisely would mean modelling a `Range[Integer | Float]` the RBS side never declares.

The Slice 5 phase 1 shape-dispatch contract MUST also satisfy:

- `Rigor::Type::Tuple` and `Rigor::Type::HashShape` MUST be projected onto their underlying nominal carriers when used as dispatch receivers. `Tuple[T1..Tn]` projects to `Nominal["Array", [union(T1..Tn)]]` (raw `Array` for empty Tuples); `HashShape{k: T,...}` projects to `Nominal["Hash", [union(constant_keys), union(values)]]` (raw `Hash` for empty shapes). The projection MUST be confined to `RbsDispatch.receiver_descriptor`; the surface contract on the carriers themselves MUST stay value-object thin.
- `Rigor::Inference::Acceptance` MUST treat shape carriers symmetrically with their projected nominal:
  - A nominal `self` MUST accept a shape `other` by projecting the shape and recursing through the existing nominal-acceptance route, so `Nominal[Array, [Integer]].accepts(Tuple[Constant[1], Constant[2]])` is equivalent to `Nominal[Array, [Integer]].accepts(Nominal[Array, [union(Constant[1], Constant[2])]])` and yields the same result.
  - A `Tuple` `self` MUST require `Tuple` `other` of equal arity, and recurse element-wise (covariant). Mismatched arity MUST collapse to `no`. Non-Tuple `other` MUST be rejected because the analyzer cannot prove arity from a generic nominal alone.
  - A `HashShape` `self` MUST require every required key of `self` to be required in `other` (depth covariant on shared entries). Optional keys on `self` MAY be absent in `other`; when present, their values MUST satisfy the same depth check. A closed `self` MUST reject known extra keys; an open `self` MAY accept wider shapes. A closed `self` MUST answer `maybe`, not `yes` or `no`, for an open-extra-key source that passes every other check: the source may hold keys the analysis does not know, or only the ones it does. `h = { a: 1 }; h.default = 0` reopens a shape whose key set is still exactly the literal's, and rejecting it reported `call.argument-type-mismatch` against `({ a: Integer }) -> void` on correct code. A missing required key, a known extra key and a refused value MUST still answer `no` for an open source. The accepted loss is a default proc that stores the keys it is asked for (`proc { |hh, k| hh[k] = 0 }`), which really adds a key the record forbids and is no longer reported. Missing required keys MUST collapse to `no`. Non-HashShape `other` MUST be rejected; nominal-side projection lives on the `accepts_nominal` route, not on the HashShape route. The one exception is a `Hash` nominal with a gradual arm — raw, or with a `Dynamic` arm in either type argument, directly or as the base of a `Difference` (`non-empty-hash[K, V]`) — which MUST answer `maybe`, as `Hash[K, V]` already answers a raw `Hash`. A hash literal with a `**splat` entry carries that arm by design (the `ExpressionTyper` hash-literal rule below), a `Hash.new` filled key by key reads as the raw `Hash`, and a declared `Hash[Symbol, untyped]` is the author saying the hash may hold anything (the reading `MutationRejoin` gives a declared gradual arm). Rejecting them reported `def.return-type-mismatch` and `call.argument-type-mismatch` on correct code: `BASE = { a: 1 }; def rec = { **BASE, b: 2 }` against `-> { a: Integer, b: Integer }`. The arm excuses only what it covers, so the answer MUST stay `no` when an argument without one rules the record out: a `K` that rejects a required key (`Hash[String, untyped]` never holds `:a`), or a `V` none of whose union members a required key's type accepts. A `Hash[K, V]` with no gradual arm MUST stay `no`, and so MUST a `Hash` subclass (`HashWithIndifferentAccess` stores String keys). The `maybe` reaches every rule that fires only on `no`, including the declaration-against-declaration override checks: an RBS override declaring `Hash[Symbol, untyped]` where the parent declares a record is no longer reported. It is no evidence for an overload, so `OverloadSelector`'s strict pass MUST NOT count a `maybe` that rests on a record's answer for such a `Hash` or for an open `HashShape`, wherever the record sits (at any depth: `Array[{ a: Integer }]`, a tuple, a union): with `({ a: Integer }) -> Integer | (Hash[Symbol, untyped]) -> String`, `{ **o, b: 2 }` has a key the closed record forbids, and taking the first overload by list position typed the call `Integer`. The `maybe` rests on it when the pair answers `no` once every record answers `no` for such a hash. A `maybe` that survives that re-ask has another source and MUST still count: with `({ a: Integer, b: Object, c: Base }) -> Integer | (Hash[Symbol, untyped]) -> String`, `{ a: 1, b: h, c: Sub.new }` answers `maybe` for `Sub`, a subclass only the Ruby source declares, whether or not `h` is open or splatted. The gradual pass still takes the record overload when no overload proves a match.
- `Rigor::Inference::RbsTypeTranslator` MUST map `RBS::Types::Tuple` and `RBS::Types::Record` to the dedicated shape carriers (NOT to `Nominal[Array]`/`Nominal[Hash]`). Element and field types MUST be translated recursively under the caller's `self_type:` / `instance_type:` / `type_vars:` context, so generics inside tuples and records survive the boundary. `Record` MUST translate required fields from `RBS::Types::Record#fields`, optional fields from `#optional_fields`, and mark the resulting `HashShape` as closed.
- `Rigor::Inference::ExpressionTyper` MUST upgrade non-empty array literals whose every element is a non-splat value to `Tuple` carriers; literals containing splats MUST keep the Slice 4 phase 2d `Nominal[Array, [union]]` form so `[*xs, 1]` still produces an inferable element type. `ExpressionTyper` MUST upgrade hash literals whose every entry is an `AssocNode` with a value-pinned scalar key — a `SymbolNode` or `StringNode` with a non-`nil` `value`/`unescaped`, an Integer, a Float, `true`, `false`, or `nil` — to `HashShape` carriers, a duplicated key keeping its first position and its last value as the runtime does; literals with `AssocSplatNode` entries or dynamic keys MUST fall through to the `Nominal[Hash, [K, V]]` form, whose `K` and `V` join every entry's key and value types. A `**splat` entry MUST contribute what the analysis reads of the hash it copies, because Ruby inserts every pair of it (a value that is not a `Hash` is converted with `to_hash` first): a `HashShape`'s keys and values; a `Hash[K, V]` nominal's type arguments, read through a `Difference` over one (`non-empty-hash[K, V]`); and the join over a union's members. Nothing else is read: not `nil` (`**nil` splats nothing since Ruby 3.4), not an anonymous `**`, an untyped or `Dynamic`-wrapped value, a raw `Hash`, or any other nominal (a `Hash` subclass's own type arguments are not `Hash`'s `K` and `V`). Every splat entry MUST also contribute `Dynamic[top]` to both sides, whatever it reads. The literal builds a new hash that no declaration describes, and `MutationRejoin` regrows a `Hash[K, V]` after a write only when it already carries a gradual arm, because it takes a precise one for a declared claim; a precise copy would therefore never grow, and `h = { **o }; h[:b] = 2; h[:b] == 2` would fold always-falsey while `h[:e] = "s"; h[:e].upcase` would fire `call.undefined-method`. The same arm covers what the analysis cannot read, including an open shape's unlisted entries and a hash filled through an alias the engine does not track, which still reads `{}`. Skipping the entry typed `o = { a: :z }; h = { **o, b: :y }` as `Hash[:b, :y]`, and `h[:a] == :z` folded always-falsey on correct code; it now types `Hash[:a | :b | Dynamic[top], :y | :z | Dynamic[top]]`. A splat MUST NOT keep a `HashShape`, even over an exact closed one: a mixed keyword hash (`f(a: 1, **h)`) is typed by this rule, and the call-site binder below declines it because it is not a shape. Integer-endpoint `RangeNode` literals MUST be carried as `Constant[Range]` so shape dispatch can resolve tuple range slices; dynamic ranges MUST remain `Nominal[Range]`.

When the receiver of a call is a `Rigor::Type::Dynamic` and no positive dispatcher tier matches, `ExpressionTyper#call_type_for` MUST return `Dynamic[top]` *silently*, without recording a `FallbackTracer` event. This is a recognised semantic outcome — the value-lattice algebra in [`value-lattice.md`](../type-specification/value-lattice.md) requires Dynamic to propagate through opaque method calls — and not a fail-soft compromise. Receivers that are not Dynamic still trigger the standard fail-soft fallback (with a tracer event) when no rule resolves.

The Slice 5 phase 2 shape-aware dispatch tier (`Rigor::Inference::MethodDispatcher::ShapeDispatch`) MUST run between the constant-folding tier and the RBS-backed tier so that `Tuple` and `HashShape` receivers resolve element-access methods to their precise per-position / per-key type rather than the projected `Array#[]` / `Hash#fetch` answer. The tier MUST handle the following catalogue, returning `nil` to defer to `RbsDispatch` when the call cannot be proved against the static shape:

- Tuple receivers: `first`, `last`, `size`/`length`/`count` (no-arg, no-block) MUST return the precise tuple element / `Constant[size]`. `[]` and `fetch` with a single `Constant[Integer]` argument MUST normalise negative indices by length and return the precise element when the index is in `[-size, size)`; out-of-range indices MUST defer (`nil`) so the projection answer applies for `fetch` (which would raise) and MUST resolve to `Constant[nil]` only when invoked through a chain step (see `dig` below). `[]` with `Constant[Range]` integer/nil endpoints and `[]` with two `Constant[Integer]` arguments MUST use Ruby `Array#[]` slice semantics and return a sliced `Tuple` or `Constant[nil]` for statically nil slices; `fetch` MUST NOT claim those forms. `dig` MUST recurse through the resolved member: a `Tuple`/`HashShape` member MUST re-dispatch into the catalogue with the remaining arguments; a `Constant[nil]` member MUST short-circuit the chain (Ruby's `Array#dig` does the same at runtime); other Constants and any non-shape carrier MUST defer so the projection answer applies. An out-of-range index that arises *during* a chain step MUST resolve to `Constant[nil]` because Ruby's `Array#dig` returns nil for out-of-range indices rather than raising.
- HashShape receivers: `size`/`length` (no-arg) MUST return `Constant[size]` only when the shape is closed and no known key is optional. `[]` and `fetch` with a single `Constant[Symbol|String]` argument MUST return the precise value when a required key is declared. Optional-key `[]`/`dig` MUST return `value | nil`, while optional-key `fetch` MUST defer because the key may be absent and Ruby would raise `KeyError`. On a **closed** shape a key outside `pairs` is provably missing and MUST resolve to `Constant[nil]` for `[]` (matching Ruby's runtime behaviour) and MUST defer for `fetch`. On an **open** shape (`extra_keys: :open`) a key outside `pairs` is *unknown*, not missing — the policy states that keys beyond `pairs` are permitted, so such a key MAY hold a value of any type — and every read of it (`[]`, `fetch`, `dig`, `values_at`) MUST resolve to `untyped`. Typing it `Constant[nil]` would be unsound in the direction that costs most: it puts `call.undefined-method` on the next line of correct code (`payload[:undeclared].upcase`). `dig` MUST follow the same chain semantics as Tuple `dig`: a single static key resolves to the precise value (or `Constant[nil]` for a key missing from a closed shape); a multi-key chain recurses with the resolved value as the new receiver, and a chain that reaches an `untyped` step MUST stay `untyped` for its remainder. `values_at` with one or more `Constant[Symbol|String]` arguments MUST return a `Tuple` whose per-position values are the per-key values, with keys missing from a closed shape filled by `Constant[nil]`. Falls through (defers) when the call has zero arguments or when any position defers under the computed-key rule below. A union of value-pinned keys that the shape all declares MUST resolve to the union of its members' per-key answers, which is exact (and `fetch` MAY answer it the same way when no member is optional). Every other key is *computed*, including a union with an undeclared member: its per-member answer on a shape the analysis saw only partly filled is the same lone `Constant[nil]` the empty-shape rule below refuses (`counts = { total: 0 }; %i[a b a].each { |k| counts[k] = counts[k] ? counts[k] + 1 : 1 }` read `counts[k]` as `nil` and reported `undefined method '+' for nil`). A computed key on a **closed** shape MUST resolve `[]`, a single-key `dig`, and its `values_at` position to the union of every declared value and `Constant[nil]`: it may match any declared key or none, and the hash reads `nil` on a miss. Deferring such a key instead let `RbsDispatch` answer the projection's nil-free value union, so `o = { a: 1 }; o[name] == 1` folded always-truthy on correct code. This covers every closed shape, including an RBS record type (`def conf: () -> { a: Integer }`; `conf[name]` is `Integer | nil`). A hash whose miss answers something else is not a closed shape by the time it is read: `default=` / `default_proc=` reopen it and `compare_by_identity` widens a shape with a key that is not identity-stable (the Hash lookup mutators below), so a computed key then defers like any open shape's. `fetch` MUST defer on a computed key, because its miss raises rather than reading nil and the projection's nil-free union is already sound for it. On an **open** shape a computed key MUST defer, since an undeclared key may hold any value, and so MUST it on the **empty** closed shape: a hash read by a computed key while it looks empty is one filled where the analysis did not see — an earlier iteration of the same loop body, a cache written through `(@cache ||= {})[key] = v`, an `instance_eval`ed file — and `Constant[nil]` there folds the read's own guard always-falsey (measured on the survey corpus: five such firings, all false positives).

This mirrors the erasure rule in [rbs-erasure.md](../type-specification/rbs-erasure.md) § *Open shapes with extra-value bounds*: the known value union constrains only the declared entries, never an unseen extra key. The rule holds past the shape tier: `RbsDispatch`'s projection of an open shape to `Hash[K, V]` (the answer for a `Union` receiver or a non-literal key) and `ContentJoin.hash_shape_key_values`' evidence from one (the seed a content join grows) MUST each carry a `Dynamic[top]` arm on the key and on the value side. Key-set-narrowing operations (`slice`, `except`, `merge`, `fetch_values`, `invert`, `size`) are already gated on `closed?` and MUST stay so — none of them can state a result whose key set is only partly known.

The shape tier MUST NOT consult the RBS environment, MUST NOT raise on any input, and MUST NOT touch the fallback tracer; it is a pure refinement layered on the type carriers. Methods outside the catalogue, non-static indices, computed keys on an open or empty shape (and in `fetch`), non-integer ranges, and any `dig`/`values_at` call whose chain steps cannot be resolved statically MUST defer so the projection-based `RbsDispatch` answer applies.

That deferred-to answer is **nil-free for a lookup that can miss**, and this is a deliberate choice rather than a gap. Core RBS declares `Hash#[]: (K) -> V` and `Array#[]: (int) -> Elem`, spelling the miss as an `%a{implicitly-returns-nil}` annotation instead of an `?` return; `RbsDispatch` reads the return type only and MUST NOT honour that annotation. Honouring it is rejected on false-positive grounds: a `Hash.new(0)` / `default_proc` receiver never returns nil, and a projected `V?` therefore fires `possible nil receiver` on correct code (measured on Rigor's own `lib`: 25 firings, every sampled one a false positive — `Hash.new { … }` and `Hash.new(0)` reads, plus `Array#max` on a provably non-empty receiver). A value union from a non-static-key `[]` on a `Hash[K, V]` nominal (or an open shape) is consequently **optimistic, not proof**: it omits nil because pessimising the common defaulted-Hash idiom costs more than the miss it would model, so `h[key]` on a `Hash[Symbol, "x" | "y"]` reading as `"x" | "y"` asserts nothing about whether the key is present. A closed `HashShape` never reaches this answer for a computed key: the shape tier resolves it with the nil arm (above), since the shape is the whole key set and nothing defaults the miss.

Certainty judgments MUST treat it that way. Per [ADR-78](../adr/78-reflexive-overfold-always-truthy.md) WD1, provable truthiness may rest only on a genuine constant, never on a fold whose soundness is conditional on a narrower form than the actual expression. A nil-free value union produced from a dynamic key is exactly that shape: sound for the keys the shape declares, applied to a read that may match none of them.

**This exclusion binds every reader of the judgment, and there are three.** `flow.always-truthy-condition` and the `&&`/`||` `constant_value_polarity` gate it shares (`StatementEvaluator#right_operand_dead?`, which both positions read) MUST NOT conclude truthiness from such a value, and MUST enforce that by consulting the mark rather than by restricting the carrier: a `Constant`-only gate is **not** sufficient, because a hash whose values share one literal type (`Hash[Symbol, 1]`) reads as a lone `Constant`, so `UNIFORM[key] || key` discards the author's fallback and `if UNIFORM[key]` fires the rule with no widening involved (issue #313). Any widening of the gate beyond `Constant` (issue #152, evaluated and declined) MUST likewise preserve this exclusion. The third reader is the `if`/`unless` branch elision — `Narrowing.predicate_certainty` as consumed by `StatementEvaluator#live_branch_for_if`/`#live_branch_for_unless`, which a value-position conditional reaches through the same evaluator — and per [ADR-101](../adr/101-optimistic-carrier-branch-elision.md) it MUST decline a verdict whose carrier is marked optimistic, for the same reason and with a wider effect: the gate above only withholds a diagnostic, whereas the elision deletes a branch the program really takes when the lookup misses.

The mark is `Rigor::Inference::OptimisticOrigin`, recorded where the dispatcher reads past the annotation and propagated as a side channel in the [ADR-75](../adr/75-dynamic-provenance.md) / [ADR-82](../adr/82-dynamic-provenance-wiring.md) sense: it MUST NOT participate in subtyping, consistency, normalization or erasure, and no diagnostic MUST fire from it. Three properties are normative. It is decided **per selected overload**, so `Array#first` is marked while `Array#first(3)` is not, and a signature that already spells the miss as `?` (`String#[]`, `Enumerable#find`) is never marked. It MUST NOT be applied to a read the shape tier resolved, because `ShapeDispatch` answers from a receiver that proves the read cannot miss (a static key on a declared shape, `first` on a non-empty `Tuple`). And the decline MUST be implemented at the consumers, never by widening `Narrowing.falsey_nominal?` / `.narrow_falsey`, which `&&=`/`||=` and the and/or surviving-left edge also read — widening the falsey fragment there re-admits `nil` into a bound local and costs `possible nil receiver` false positives.

**The exclusion binds the predicate a consumer actually reads, not only the bare carrier.** A guard is normally written `v.nil?`, `!v.nil?` or `v.nil? || w.nil?`, and each of those folds to a `Constant` of its own; treating that `Constant` as unmarked laundered the optimism straight through the gates (issue #313). Resolving the mark for an expression MUST therefore derive it through the folds that restate the carrier's nil-ness: an argument-free, blockless `nil?` or `!` call resolves to its receiver's mark, a blockless `==` / `!=` / `eql?` / `equal?` / `===` with exactly one argument where exactly one operand is the `nil` literal (`v == nil`, `nil != v`) resolves to the other operand's mark (issue [#1094](https://github.com/rigortype/rigor/issues/1094) — the same statement as `v.nil?`, spelled as a comparison), an `&&` / `||` node to either operand's, a single-statement parenthesised body to that statement's, a safe-navigation call `v&.m` to its receiver's mark (it is `nil` whenever `v` is, so it restates `v`'s presence; Ruby skips that one call and no more, so a plain call chained after it, `v&.m.n`, is sent to the `nil` and is not derived — a nil-collapsing predicate over it, `v&.m.nil?`, still is, through the rule above), and a local / ivar bound to any of those carries it onward exactly as a bare copy does. A multiple assignment carries it onward too, slot by slot (§ Multi-Target Binder): `k, v = h[key]` binds `nil` to both names on a miss. The derivation MUST stop there: a value predicate (`empty?`, `zero?`, `any?`), and a comparison against anything but the `nil` literal, folds from the carrier's *value* rather than its nil-ness, and deriving through it would widen this channel into a general taint that silences honest verdicts. An element read on the carrier — `v.last`, `v[1]`, or any call `NilClass` does not define — MUST NOT be derived either, for a different reason: on a miss it raises `NoMethodError` instead of producing a value, so the value it does produce exists only when the carrier was present and its nil-freeness rests on the method's own answer (a `Tuple` slot is proof). `n = pairs.first.last ? 1 : "none"` and `n = pairs.first&.last.abs ? 1 : "none"` therefore still elide their else arm; the program raises on the miss before `n` is bound. Not derived today, with no measured demand: a block parameter destructuring a marked value (`h[key].then { |a, b| … }`), a class predicate that a miss flips (`v.is_a?(String)`), and a call `NilClass` does define, whose miss answers something other than a raise (`v.itself`, `v.to_s`). Known over-derivation: an `||` value fallback (`h[key] || default`) resolves to the left operand's mark although a miss yields `default`, so its binding, and since the destructure rule its slots, decline on a nil-freeness that is really the default's ([#1299](https://github.com/rigortype/rigor/issues/1299)). One resolver MUST own the judgment for all three consumers, since a per-consumer copy is what let them disagree.

**The fold must also not stand as the predicate's own type.** The derivation above reaches only consumers that can see the AST and the scope's mark table; a folded `Constant[false]` on `v.nil?` additionally travels where the mark cannot — into the enclosing method's return summary, so a helper ending in `!h[k].nil?` published `Constant[true]`, and a caller's `if helper(…)` reported `flow.always-truthy-condition` on a guard a missing key makes live (issue [#1172](https://github.com/rigortype/rigor/issues/1172); the three `lib/` firings are the same shape — two call sites of `raise_duck_exception?` in `check_rules.rb` and one of `nil_class_defines?` in `expression_typer.rb`). A call whose mark is *derived through* the predicate folds above MUST therefore NOT type as `Constant[true]` / `Constant[false]`; `ExpressionTyper` widens that answer to `bool` (`Constant[true] | Constant[false]`) at the call's type funnel. The widening MUST go only toward what a miss really answers (`OptimisticOrigin.miss_answer`): the carrier — the marked read, or a safe-navigation call over a marked receiver — answers `nil` on a miss, and each nil-collapsing predicate over it answers what it answers for that value (`!` the negation, `nil?` / `== nil` whether it is `nil`, `!= nil` the reverse; a single-statement parenthesised body passes through). A folded boolean widens when the miss answers the other boolean, or when the answer cannot be told (an `&&` / `||`, a binding that recorded none); otherwise it stays, since widening would invent a value no run produces. A local or ivar read answers what its binding recorded ([#1302](https://github.com/rigortype/rigor/issues/1302)): a write that marks its target MUST record the right-hand side's miss answer beside the mark (`Scope#with_optimistic_local` / `#with_optimistic_ivar`'s `miss:`), so the bound form widens exactly as the inline form does — `x = v&.empty?; !x` keeps its `true`, while `v = h[k]; v.nil?` records `nil` and still widens. The answer lives in the mark's own table entry, so every transition that keeps the mark keeps it (`Scope#with_mutated_local`, a loop's rebind across iterations) and every one that drops the mark drops it; a loop iteration's own mark comes without an answer, so once one joins the binding the answer cannot be told. A control-flow join keeps a name's answer only when every arm that marks it agrees, and an arm that leaves the name unmarked contributes nothing, since a miss runs only through the marked arms. A multiple assignment records `nil` in every slot it marks when each marked part of the right-hand side answers `nil` on a miss — the short-array bet and a `nil` destructure both pad with `nil` — and nothing otherwise (`a, b = !h[k]` binds the miss's `true` to `a` alone). So `v&.m?` keeps its `Constant[true]` (a miss answers `nil`) and `!v&.empty?` keeps its `true` (`!false` on a hit, `!nil` on a miss), while `v&.m.nil?` and `!!h[k]`, which a miss flips, widen; the certainty consumers decline on the mark in every case. A cause recorded on the call node itself is the carrier's own mark, and the carrier's type is unchanged — `h[k]` still reads nil-free even when `V` is a literal `true`/`false` — and a predicate over a proof-carrying receiver still folds: the gate is the derived mark, not the predicate's shape.

Carrier shape MUST NOT be used as a proxy for this judgment. A `Hash` whose values share one type yields a lone `Constant`, which is exactly as optimistic as the union case; conversely most non-`Constant` carriers reaching a verdict are genuine proofs. Measured over eleven projects: 47 of 2,060 verdicts rest on the optimistic mark, while a non-`Constant` shape gate would decline 134 and still miss 12 of the 47 (`docs/notes/20260806-issue-286-optimistic-carrier-provenance-census.md`).

This split is normative: implementations MUST NOT define operator-method-aware subclasses of any `Rigor::Type` form (for example, a hypothetical `Rigor::Type::IntegerType` carrying `+`/`*` rules). Operator semantics MUST be expressed as method-handler entries that the dispatcher consults; specialising the type class for built-in arithmetic is rejected to keep the type lattice and method semantics independently extensible.

## Local Variables

Local variable read nodes (`Prism::LocalVariableReadNode`) MUST be looked up in the receiver scope. A bound name MUST return the bound `Rigor::Type`. An unbound name MUST fail soft to `Dynamic[top]` per the rule above; `Scope#type_of` MUST NOT raise on unbound locals.

Local variable write nodes (`Prism::LocalVariableWriteNode` and the targets that imply it) MUST be typed as the type of their value expression. Binding the result back into the scope is the responsibility of the statement-level evaluator (see Slice 3 in [`docs/adr/4-type-inference-engine.md`](../adr/4-type-inference-engine.md)); `Scope#type_of` itself MUST NOT mutate the scope.

## Statement-Level Evaluation

`Rigor::Scope#type_of` is a pure expression-level query and MUST NOT thread scope. The statement-level evaluator `Rigor::Inference::StatementEvaluator` (Slice 3 phase 2) sits next to it and provides the complementary scope-threading surface. Its public delegate on `Rigor::Scope` MUST exist:

```ruby
Rigor::Scope#evaluate(node, tracer: nil) #=> [Rigor::Type, Rigor::Scope]
```

The contract MUST satisfy:

- The first element of the returned pair MUST be the type that `node` produces, equivalent to what `Scope#type_of(node)` would return for a pure expression. The second element MUST be the scope observable AFTER `node` has run; for nodes that perform no scope effect this MUST be the receiver scope (compared with `==`, the receiver's identity MAY differ).
- The receiver scope MUST never be mutated. Internal recursion MUST allocate fresh `StatementEvaluator` instances for every forked scope so branches stay isolated and the equality of distinct branch outputs is observable.
- The `tracer:` keyword MUST be threaded into every nested `Scope#type_of` call so fail-soft fallbacks emitted while typing children of a statement-y node are recorded under the same tracer.
- An `evaluate` call against a node that the evaluator does not specialise MUST defer to `Scope#type_of(node, tracer:)` and MUST return the receiver scope unchanged. This preserves the Slice 1 fail-soft policy: an unrecognised statement-y node MUST NOT raise.

The catalogue of nodes that the evaluator MUST recognise in Slice 3 phase 2 is:

- `Prism::ProgramNode` and `Prism::StatementsNode` — sequential evaluation that threads scope through every child statement in declaration order. The body's value MUST be the type of the last statement (or `Constant[nil]` for an empty body); the post-scope MUST be the post-scope of the last statement.
- `Prism::LocalVariableWriteNode` — evaluates the rvalue under the entry scope and binds `name` to the resulting type via `Scope#with_local`. The pair's type MUST equal the rvalue type.
- `Prism::MultiWriteNode` (Slice 5 phase 2 sub-phase 2) — evaluates the right-hand side once under the entry scope, then walks the destructuring tree (`lefts` / `rest` / `rights`, with `rest` a `Prism::SplatNode` whose `expression` MAY be a `Prism::LocalVariableTargetNode`) and binds every named local through `Rigor::Inference::MultiTargetBinder`. The pair's type MUST equal the right-hand side's type (matching Ruby's `(a, b = [1, 2]) #=> [1, 2]` semantics). When the right-hand side is a `Type::Tuple`, per-slot bindings MUST be element-wise: missing front/back slots collapse to `Constant[nil]`, the rest slot binds to a `Type::Tuple` of the middle elements (`Tuple[]` when the source has no surplus). An `Array[T]` right-hand side (including a `Refined` / `Difference` over one) binds each fixed slot to `T` and the rest to `Array[T]`, and every fixed slot it binds MUST also be recorded through `Scope#with_optimistic_local` (issue [#1093](https://github.com/rigortype/rigor/issues/1093); § Multi-Target Binder below). A right-hand side that is itself optimistically nil-free (`k, v = pairs.first`), or a literal one with such an element (`x, y = pairs.first, 1`), MUST hand its mark to the fixed slots it fills, by the same section's statement-level rule. A union right-hand side distributes over its members, and a value that provably has no implicit `to_ary` binds as the one-element tuple `[rhs]` (issue [#1094](https://github.com/rigortype/rigor/issues/1094); same section). Other right-hand sides bind every slot to `Dynamic[top]`. Nested `Prism::MultiTargetNode` targets MUST recurse through the same binder. Instance-variable targets (`@a, *@rest = xs`) MUST decompose by the same rules and bind through `Scope#with_ivar`, each optimistic slot recorded through `Scope#with_optimistic_ivar` — the mark a single `@x = xs.first` write already records (issue [#1110](https://github.com/rigortype/rigor/issues/1110)). An index target (`h[:a], z = 1, 2`, nested or splatted too) stores its slot through `[]=` on its receiver, so once the bindings are applied the evaluator MUST widen every variable the receiver can evaluate to through `IndexWriteWidening.widen` — the `MutationWidening` seam a plain `h[:a] = v` takes — with `[index_type..., slot_type]` as the argument types, where `slot_type` is the value `MultiTargetBinder` reports for that target in `Result#index_targets`, so the slot's value joins as content evidence (issue [#560](https://github.com/rigortype/rigor/issues/560)). The slot is softened as a local in the same position is (ADR-57). A stored value carries no optimistic mark, but it needs none: the straight-line join always adds the `Dynamic[top]` floor, so no fold can rest on the dropped `nil`, whereas joining the `nil` fires `call.possible-nil-receiver` on the correlated guard the softening exists for (`r[:k], r[:v] = h.find { … }; r[:v].upcase if r[:k]`). After each target's widening the evaluator MUST drop the indexed narrowing the store overwrites through `IndexedNarrowing.invalidate_indexed_write`, the invalidation a `[]=` call takes: the `(receiver, key)` entry of the first index argument when it is a literal key, and nothing otherwise. Otherwise the widening carries the narrowing across its rebind and `m[:a] ||= "d"; m[:a], y = 1, 2` keeps reading `"d"`. Without it the literal survived the store: `h = { a: 0 }; h[:a], z = 1, 2; h[:a] == 0` folded to `true` on a hash whose slot holds `1`. The widening MUST run after the bindings, because Ruby evaluates a target's receiver before it assigns any target: in `h, h[:a] = h, 1` the store lands on the object `h` ends up bound to, and widening first would let that binding restore the literal. `ScopeIndexer`'s class-ivar pre-pass MUST likewise count an `@h[k]` target as a `[]=` mutation of `@h`, as it counts `@h[k] ||= v`, so a sibling method reads the widened seed. The same target also stores outside a multi-assign, as a `for` index and as a rescue reference; the `Prism::ForNode` and `Prism::BeginNode` bullets below bind those, and the pre-pass counts them alike. Other non-local targets (class/global variables, constants, call targets, anonymous splat) MUST be silently skipped.
- `Prism::IfNode` and `Prism::UnlessNode` — evaluate the predicate first (its post-scope is shared by both branches), then evaluate each branch under the post-predicate scope. The result type MUST be the union of the two branch types; the post-scope MUST be the join-with-nil-injection of the two branch scopes (see below). A `nil` branch (no else / no then) MUST contribute `Constant[nil]` and the post-predicate scope.
- `Prism::ElseNode` — evaluates its body under the receiver scope, returning `[Constant[nil], scope]` for empty bodies.
- `Prism::CaseNode` and `Prism::CaseMatchNode` — evaluate the predicate first; every `WhenNode`/`InNode` body and the optional else-clause are evaluated independently under the post-predicate scope and merged with the same join-with-nil-injection rule generalised to N branches.
- `Prism::WhenNode` and `Prism::InNode` — evaluate their statements under the receiver scope; an empty body MUST be `[Constant[nil], scope]`.
- `Prism::BeginNode` — evaluate the primary path (body, then optional else-clause; the else-clause MUST replace the body's value while the body's scope effects still apply because the body did run before the else). Each `Prism::RescueNode` in the chain is an alternative exit path evaluated under the entry scope. A local reference (`rescue TypeError => e`) MUST bind the exception instance type in that arm's entry scope: the union of the named classes' instances, with `StandardError` for a bare `rescue` and `Dynamic[top]` for a named class that does not resolve. An index-target reference (`rescue => h[:e]`) binds no name: it stores the exception through `[]=` on its receiver, so the arm MUST instead widen the receiver through `IndexWriteWidening.widen` with `[index_type..., exception_type]`, the index typed in the arm's entry scope, and drop the stored slot's indexed narrowing, exactly as `rescue => e; h[:e] = e` does. Other reference kinds (`rescue => @err`) are not bound yet (issue [#1217](https://github.com/rigortype/rigor/issues/1217)). Without it `h = { e: 0 }; begin … rescue => h[:e]; end; h[:e] == 0` folded to `true` on a hash whose slot holds the exception. The exit type MUST be the union of the primary and rescue exits; the exit scope MUST be the join-with-nil-injection of the primary and rescue scopes. A rescue arm that never falls through contributes neither: one ending in `return`, `next`, `break`, or a receiverless `raise` / `throw` / `exit` / `abort` / `fail`, and one whose type is `bot` — an arm ending in `retry`, or in `tries < 3 ? retry : raise`, leaves back into the primary body, never past the `begin`. When a `retry` in the rescue chain targets this `begin` (B2.1) — one not inside a nested `rescue` clause, a rescue modifier's fallback (Ruby 4.0.5 retries the modifier's own expression), or a nested block, lambda, `def` or class body — control re-enters the primary body carrying every rebind made before it, so the primary path and the rescue chain MUST be re-evaluated under an entry widened by the scopes on the retry edge. On the arm's side those are the scope at each such `retry` the chain reaches, and the post-scope of each arm holding one: a guarded `retry if delay < 8` leaves through its truthy edge while its fall-through may be dead (holding `delay` as `bot`), and an `ensure` the `retry` leaves through, or a `retry` the evaluator only types (`log(tries < 5 ? retry : :gave_up)`), shows only in the arm's post-scope. On the primary body's side they are the scope after each statement of the `begin`'s own frame and the body's exit scope: a statement of a nested block or lambda body is not in the frame (a block parameter can shadow the counter), and the else-clause is not a raise point, because an exception it raises is not rescued by this `begin`. The post-statement scope carries a rebind on a branch that then raises (`if bad; tries += 1; raise; end`), and a write threaded into the raising call's own operands (`raise Retry.new(tries += 1) if flaky?`). A local or instance variable bound in the entry scope widens when a scope on the edge binds it to a type the accumulated binding does not already accept; a binding that carries `Dynamic` anywhere (`[fetch]`) is accepted only by one with a `Dynamic` member, since `Array[Integer]` gradually accepts `Array[untyped]` but keeping it would claim the elements are still Integers. The acceptance test keeps a narrowing from widening: inside `log if m == :fast`, or at `retry if m == :fast`, the scope holds `m: :fast`, which the entry's `:fast | :slow` accepts, and widening it would turn a declared `:fast | :slow` return into `Symbol`. A name the entry does not bind joins the edge only from a retrying arm, and only when the primary body never writes it; a local that joins widens to `nil` joined with its rebind, since it is nil on the first entry and after a body that never raised, and an instance variable to the rebind alone, its first-entry value being unknown rather than nil. A name the body writes MUST stay unbound on the edge, reading `Dynamic[top]` as on the first entry: binding a type would claim it set even when the body raised before assigning it, folding the arm's `conn.close if conn` to always-truthy for the body's connection, or to always-falsey for the arm's own `conn = nil`. The widening MUST first keep literals, joining the accumulated binding with each rebind, and hold when one re-evaluation under it puts nothing new on the edge: `st = :ok; begin; st = :retrying; …; st = :ok; rescue; retry; end` stays `:ok | :retrying`, and `ok = false … ok = true` stays `false | true`, which a declared `bool` accepts. When the re-evaluation moves the edge again — a counter's `0 | 1` meets `2` — the entry MUST instead be widened to the Nominal envelope (`Constant` → `Nominal[<class>]`, `Tuple` → `Array`, `HashShape` → `Hash`) of everything both passes saw, and evaluated once more; `nil`, `true` and `false` stay constants there, being their classes' only values. Without the primary-body rebinds, `tries = 0; begin; tries += 1; raise if tries < 3; rescue; retry; end` folded `tries < 3` to always-truthy although it is false on the third attempt. Known limitation: the envelope pass is taken as converged rather than checked, so a self-referential rebind (`x = [x]`) is not re-absorbed, and a literal set that needs more than one re-evaluation to close widens to its envelope. When an `ensure_clause` is present, its scope effects MUST be layered on the joined exit scope, so locals bound exclusively in the ensure stay observable; the ensure's value MUST NOT contribute to the exit type.
- `Prism::WhileNode` and `Prism::UntilNode` — evaluate the predicate (its post-scope is observable in the body), then evaluate the body. The predicate is evaluated once, from the pre-loop scope, although it runs before every iteration, so a variable it writes MUST be widened past its value pin (`Type::Combinator.widen_value_pinned`) when a later evaluation can store something else: when the predicate reads the variable it writes (`i = 0; while (i += 1) < 3; end`, whose pinned `1` met the exit edge `i >= 3` and left `i` as `bot`) or reads a variable the body writes (`while check(k = i * 2); i += 1; end`). A write that reads neither keeps its binding (`until line = (flag ? "x" : nil)` still exits on `"x"`). The result type MUST be `Constant[nil]`. The base post-scope MUST be the join-with-nil-injection of the post-predicate scope and the single-pass exit scope, modelling "body might have run zero or more times". [ADR-56](../adr/56-block-captured-local-mutation.md) slice B overlays a capped body-fixpoint result on that base for every local the body can rebind (`loop_rebind_fixpoint`, sharing the `Inference::BodyFixpoint` helper, cap 3, final `Constant→Nominal` widen, `Dynamic[top]` floor on non-convergence): a pre-existing local seeds with its post-predicate binding, a local first assigned inside the body seeds with `Constant[nil]` for the zero-iteration path, and the predicate's loop-entry edge is re-narrowed each iteration. A loop body that rebinds no local is byte-identical to the single-pass base. Every body evaluation — the single pass and each fixpoint pass (`StatementEvaluator#loop_iteration`) — MUST end with an exit scope that joins (with nil-injection) the fall-through scope and the scope at every `next` that targets the loop, collected as the "Block jump paths" bullet below describes: a `next` returns to the predicate, so its scope is one of the iteration's exits and feeds the next iteration, and a local first bound on a `next` path reads `T | nil` rather than vanishing. A `next` MUST NOT disable the predicate's exit-edge narrowing; a `break` does. The continuation MUST union each rebound local's binding at every `break` that targets the loop, read from a pass whose entry is the converged binding — the fixpoint's last pass when it stabilised (or, on the display path, the converged re-record pass), otherwise one more pass from the converged binding that MUST NOT record into the per-node scope index; a `return` that pass reaches joins the method's return type as any pass's does. The arms MUST NOT come from the single pass alone, which runs before any loop-carried rebind has moved and from an entry the predicate has not narrowed, with one exception: a `begin … end while` / `until` body runs once before the predicate is tested, outside every narrowed entry, so its single pass's `break` scopes MUST be joined as well. A `break` binding MUST NOT feed another iteration, and a name whose continuation binding is `Dynamic[top]` — the fixpoint's floor, or an already untyped local — MUST keep it. `w = String.new; i = 0; while i < 3; i += 1; if i.odd?; w = i; next; end; end` therefore types `w` as `Integer | String`, never `String`, and `flag = false; i = 0; while i < 5; i += 1; break(flag = true) if i == 2; end` types `flag` as `bool`, never `false`.
- `Prism::ForNode` — evaluate the collection once, then bind the index in the post-collection scope to the per-iteration element type (the collection's structural element type, else the block parameters the dispatcher reads off its `each`), evaluate the body once under that binding (a `for` has no rebind fixpoint), and join the no-iteration post-collection scope with the body's exit scope — its fall-through joined with every `next` that targets the loop, as `eval_loop`'s passes are (join-with-nil-injection; `for` opens no variable scope, so body locals leak) — then join the `break`-path scopes the fall-through drops. With no fixpoint that one pass is also the only source of `break` scopes, so a `break` whose branch only a loop-carried rebind reaches is still lost. The result type is `Constant[nil]`, the `while` / `until` policy; Ruby's `for` evaluates to the collection, a known gap (issue [#1216](https://github.com/rigortype/rigor/issues/1216)). A local index binds with `Scope#with_local`; a multi-target index (`for a, b in pairs`) MUST go through `MultiTargetBinder` exactly as a `Prism::MultiWriteNode` does, `Result#apply_to` included. An index target — the whole index (`for h[:a] in xs`), a slot of a multi-target index (`for h[:a], w in pairs`, from `Result#index_targets`) or a bare splat's expression (`for *h[:a] in pairs`, stored as `Dynamic[top]`, the binder's floor for a rest it cannot decompose) — stores the element or its slot through `[]=` at the top of every iteration, so the index binding MUST widen its receiver through `IndexWriteWidening.widen` with `[index_type..., stored_type]`, after any bindings, and drop the stored slot's indexed narrowing, as the `Prism::MultiWriteNode` bullet requires. The widening MUST happen before the body, which reads the widened receiver, and the post-loop join MUST keep it beside the no-iteration literal, the answer a body store `for x in xs; h[:a] = x; end` gives. Without it `h = { a: 0 }; for h[:a] in [1, 2]; end; h[:a] == 0` folded to `true` on a hash whose slot holds `2`. Other index kinds (an instance variable, a bare `*name` splat) are not bound yet (issue [#1217](https://github.com/rigortype/rigor/issues/1217)).
- `Prism::AndNode` and `Prism::OrNode` — evaluate the LHS, then the RHS under the LHS's *narrowed* entry edge (the truthy edge for `&&`, the falsey edge for `||`; the Slice 6 rule, normative in § StatementEvaluator integration below). The result type MUST be `union(narrow_falsey(left_type), right_type)` for `&&` and `union(narrow_truthy(left_type), right_type)` for `||` — only the operand's short-circuiting fragment can be the node's value, so `s || full` with `s : String?` types `String | <full>` rather than re-admitting the stripped `nil`. When the left operand is a `Type::Constant` whose polarity makes the RHS dead (`false && b`, `1 || b`), the result type MUST be the left operand alone; this `constant_value_polarity` short-circuit MUST stay `Constant`-only and MUST decline a left operand whose `OptimisticOrigin` resolves against the LHS post-scope (issue #313, below). The dead RHS is still evaluated and its post-scope still joins, so only its value is dropped. The post-scope MUST be the join-with-nil-injection of the LHS and RHS post-scopes (modelling "LHS always ran; RHS only sometimes ran"), except when the RHS provably terminates (`a or raise`), where control never reaches a successor through the RHS edge and both the type and the scope MUST be the surviving LHS edge alone. As a predicate — an `if` / `unless` condition, or the left operand of another `&&` / `||` — the operator's truthy and falsey edges are normally `Narrowing.predicate_scopes` of that joined post-scope. That scope also holds the path that skipped the RHS, so a local the RHS binds reads `nil` there, and narrowing cannot tell that on an `&&`'s truthy edge, and an `||`'s falsey edge, the RHS certainly ran. When the RHS, or the RHS of an `&&` / `||` on the left, holds a write or jump (`Inference::OperandEffects`), `StatementEvaluator#eval_with_edges` MUST instead build the edges from the scopes the operands left. The edge on which the RHS certainly ran (`&&`'s truthy one, `||`'s falsey one) MUST be the RHS's own edge, with the instance variables and globals of the whole operator's narrowing over the scope the RHS left laid over it (`StatementEvaluator#ran_edge`). That puts back what the LHS narrowed and a call in the RHS reset, as the joined-scope narrowing always did: the class's narrowed instance variables and the regex globals (`@parent && (node = find_node)`, `line =~ re && (k = Integer($2))`). No call resets a local, so every local MUST keep the RHS edge's binding and marks, and no variable the operator's operands write may be overlaid: re-narrowing one can contradict its new value (`x.nil? && log(x = "d") && ok` read `x` as `bot`) or drop the marks its write stamped (`m = AppConfig::MODE`'s published-constant mark, `r = @right`'s declaration-sourced one). The other edge MUST be the join-with-nil-injection of the LHS's and the RHS's edges of that polarity. A terminating RHS keeps the narrowing of the surviving edge. A provably-live `if` / `unless` branch MUST run on the same edge rather than on the un-narrowed post-predicate scope. `if a && xs.size > (n = f)` therefore reads `n` without `nil` in its body, while its `else` keeps the `nil` of the path where `a` was falsey. An operator without such a RHS keeps the joined-scope narrowing exactly.
- `Prism::ParenthesesNode` — threads scope through the inner expression so `(x = 1; x + 2)` binds `x` and produces `Constant[3]`.
- `Prism::CallNode` operands (issue [#1223](https://github.com/rigortype/rigor/issues/1223)) — Ruby evaluates a call's receiver, then its arguments left to right, then a block-pass argument, and only then runs the method. The evaluator MUST thread, in that order, every operand that holds a variable write or a `next` / `break` targeting the construct around the call (`Inference::OperandEffects`). A local write counts only when its `depth` reaches past every block and lambda it is nested in within the operand, so `puts(xs.map { |x| y = x })` counts nothing and `puts(xs.each { t = 1 })` counts the outer `t`. Every instance-variable, class-variable and global write counts, and so does an index `||=` / `&&=` / `op=`. An in-place mutation counts on the same terms as a write (`OperandEffects.outliving_mutation?`): a call to a name the straight-line widening responds to (`MutationWidening::SHAPE_MUTATORS`) whose receiver's `ReceiverAlias.mutated_reads`, or the local an element read is rooted at, reach past every block and lambda between the call and the operand's root; an instance variable, class variable or global always does, and an `it` read only outside every such block. Threading the call runs its post-call effects — the receiver widening, the ADR-56 block write-back and content join, the indexed and method-chain narrowing drops — so a chained `b.push(2).size`, an argument `puts(b.push(2))`, a literal element `[g.push(2)]`, an element read `puts(c[0] << 5)`, an index store `puts(h[:x] = 2)` and `xs.each { |x| h << x }.size` widen the variable as the same call does as a statement; before this, an operand was typed as a pure value and `b` kept the `[1]` its literal wrote. A mutator on a variable the operand's own block introduces is that block's to widen. One gap remains: the outer call of a self-returning chain (`v << 2 << 3`) names no variable, so only the inner call's evidence is joined, a precision gap the join's `Dynamic[top]` floor keeps from reporting. A `def`, class or module body and a `defined?` are not looked into. An operand with a handler MUST be evaluated through it. An argument list, keyword hash, association, splat, block-pass, array or hash literal, interpolation, embedded statement or range MUST thread its children in child order. A safe-navigation call skips its arguments when the receiver is nil, so the scope after them MUST be joined (join-with-nil-injection) with the scope after the receiver. When no operand holds a write, jump or in-place mutation, the call MUST be evaluated exactly as without this rule. Otherwise the call's block entry, its ADR-56 write-back and every post-call effect MUST run from the scope the operands left. The call's value and every typing of its own receiver and arguments MUST still read the scope the operands started from (`StatementEvaluator#operand_scope`): the block's expected parameter types, the closure-escape classification, the macro `self` narrowing, and a content mutator's argument types. `n = 0; out = []; out << (n += 1)` therefore binds `n` to `1` and appends `1`, never the `2` a typing after the write reads. The one exception is a later operand (issue [#1256](https://github.com/rigortype/rigor/issues/1256)): an operand the threading enters from a scope an earlier operand moved — an argument after one that writes or mutates, an argument after a receiver that does, a literal element or hash value after such an element — MUST be recorded into the per-node scope index with that scope and typed from it (`Inference::OperandWalk`), and every typing of the call or literal, and of its operands, MUST read that value (`OperandWalk.type_of`, `ExpressionTyper`'s `operand_types:`). So `puts(b.unshift("s"), b.first.upcase)` reads `b` as the `unshift` left it, as the two statements split do, and `[n += 1, n += 1]` types as `[1, 2]`; the operand holding a write is still typed from its own entry. The comparison is against the scope the nearest enclosing such operand is typed from, and each is typed once, bottom-up, holding the values of those below it, so an operator chain whose every operand writes types each operand once rather than once per level. A threaded call below the root still contributes no value of its own; its later operands join the root's walk and are typed as soon as they are all taken, so the helpers its invocation runs read them, and the root does not type them again. A position `ExpressionTyper` gives no value of its own or reads through (an argument list, an association, a splat, a block-pass, a keyword hash, whose `**h` form reads the splatted value directly) is recorded but not typed, and its children are compared in turn, with or without an effect of their own. A `rescue` modifier's arm is threaded outside the walk and keeps the modifier's entry in the index: its entry nil-injects a local the expression first binds, the sound join (the raise may come before the write), but reading the arm there made `Float(u = s) rescue u.strip` report a possible nil receiver where the raise almost always comes from `Float`, after the write. Keeping the arm where it was before this rule trades that rare path for no false positive on the common one (ADR-5). An evaluator an operand opens — for a handler-evaluated operand, or for a block a threaded call runs — carries the index's recorder separately from its own `on_enter` (`StatementEvaluator`'s `operand_recorder`), so a call or literal inside it records its own later operands and nothing else; an unrecorded pass (a converged loop's or block's break pass, an `ensure` replayed per jump, a jump's arguments) records no later operand. `call.argument-type-mismatch` types each argument from its own index entry, so `(k = 0; 5)[k]` checks `k` as `0`. A rule that types a whole operand expression from its own index entry (`dump_type([n += 1, n += 1])`, an argument-type check on a literal argument) still types the later elements inside it from that entry; the binding the expression leaves (`pair = [n += 1, n += 1]`) carries the threaded value. A later operand of a call whose receiver is a safe-navigation call reads the join of that call's skipped and run arguments, although Ruby skips the whole chain when the receiver is nil: `a&.push(d = 1).push(d.even?)` reads `d` as `1 | nil`. A call threaded as another call's operand MUST contribute its scope effects without being typed, and neither it nor any call inside an operand the evaluator threads through a handler (the `strict?` in `opts[:k] = strict? ? q.shift : nil`, the `ready?` in `puts(ready? && @items.push(1))`; `StatementEvaluator`'s `in_operand`, carried into every evaluator an operand evaluator opens) MUST apply the post-return narrowing (RBS::Extended, plugin and RSpec-matcher assertions), the intervening-call reset of the class's narrowed instance variables, or a regex-global reset of its own: a call in an operand never applied them before this rule, and applying them only when the operand happens to write made `$stdout.puts(Integer(v = $2)); $1.upcase` report where `$stdout.puts(Integer($2))` does not. The regex-global reset of an operand call is applied by the statement that holds it instead, for every call in its operands whether the evaluator threads them or not, and only when that call is known to match ([#1365](https://github.com/rigortype/rigor/issues/1365); `MatchRebinding.value_may_rebind?`, see the `subject =~ pattern` bullet below). The reading says whether the call alone may rebind the slot, an over-approximation of what it does that no surrounding write changes, so an operand's answer does not depend on whether it writes: `Integer` runs no match, and `$stdout.puts(Integer(v = $2))` keeps `$1` narrowed as `$stdout.puts(Integer($2))` does. The instance-variable reset stays off for operand calls ([#1257](https://github.com/rigortype/rigor/issues/1257)). Nested literals, statement lists and `(…)` in an operand MUST be threaded child by child without typing them, so neither a deep literal nor an operator chain whose operands all write re-types its subtree once per level. So `out << (n += 1)`, `(seen += 1) == 2`, `puts(g = e)` inside a block, `h[s = 1]`, `foo(k: (s = 1))` and `@x = foo(s = 1)` all leave the write in the post-statement scope. `g = nil; 1.upto(g = 2) { h = g }` reads `g` as `2` in the block, and `g = 0; 1.upto(g = 2) { g = :z }` types `g` as `2 | :z`: the write-back seeds from the argument's write, not from the pre-call binding. A later operand that reads an earlier operand's write (`[n += 1, n += 1]`, `foo(a = 1, a)`) is typed after that write, as above; the call's own block is still typed from the entry scope for the call's value (`(g = 1).then { g }` reads `g`'s pre-write binding there), although the evaluator runs it, and records it, from the scope the operands left. A predicate call whose receiver is a local or instance-variable write, bare or parenthesised on its own (`(x = e).nil?`, `(x ||= e).empty?`, `(@x = e).is_a?(C)`), MUST narrow that variable exactly as the same call on a read of it does (`Narrowing.write_receiver_as_read`). The write's value is the binding it leaves, and the predicate is narrowed in the scope after the write. Without this, the variable the receiver now binds kept its `nil` on the edge that rules it out, and `(args = node.arguments&.arguments).nil? || args.empty?` reported a possible nil receiver. For the same reason `Inference::ReceiverAlias.candidates` MUST answer such a write, bare or parenthesised, with a read of the variable it writes, so a mutator on it (`(buf ||= []) << x`) widens that variable; `ReceiverAlias.mutated_reads` MUST answer a class-variable or global write the same way when it is the receiver itself (`(@@c ||= []) << x`), on the terms it takes a direct class-variable or global read (issue #587 (a) below). A mutator whose receiver is the element an indexed `||=` narrowing records — `h[k] << x`, `h[k][:x] = v`, or `(h[k] ||= []) << x`, whose value is that element — MUST widen the recorded slot as the mutator widens that value, or drop it when the widening declines (`IndexedNarrowing#widen_mutated_slot`). Otherwise `groups[:a] ||= []; groups[:a] << 1; groups[:a].size == 1` folds always-falsey. The one exception is a String mutator on a slot every member of which is a String form (a literal, `String`, or a refinement over it): the widening declines a `String` nominal and does not model a refinement, but the mutator rewrites the same object in place, so the slot stays non-nil and the narrowing MUST be kept, floored to `String`. Dropping it read `h[:name]` back as a declared `String?` after `h[:name] ||= default; h[:name].strip!` and reported a nil receiver on correct code.
- `Prism::ArrayNode`, `Prism::HashNode`, `Prism::InterpolatedStringNode`, `Prism::InterpolatedSymbolNode`, `Prism::InterpolatedXStringNode` and `Prism::RangeNode` — the value MUST be the one `Scope#type_of` gives at the entry scope. A literal that holds a write or jump (the `Prism::CallNode` rule above) MUST thread its children in child order, so `s = 0; [s = 1]` and `"#{s += 1}"` leave `s` at `1`. A regexp interpolation is not threaded: under its `o` flag the parts run only once.
- `Prism::RescueModifierNode` — `expr rescue alt` that holds a write or jump MUST thread `expr`. It MUST evaluate `alt` from the entry scope joined (join-with-nil-injection) with the scope `expr` leaves, since the raise may come after some of `expr`'s writes. It MUST answer the join-with-nil-injection of the scopes `expr` and `alt` leave, or the scope `expr` leaves alone when `alt` unconditionally exits (`rescue next`). `s = 0; x = foo rescue (s = 1)` therefore types `s` as `0 | 1`. The value MUST be the one `Scope#type_of` gives.
- `Prism::ClassNode` and `Prism::ModuleNode` — evaluate the body in a *fresh* scope (Ruby's class/module scope does NOT see the outer locals; it shares only the Environment). The body's value is the body's last statement (or `Constant[nil]` for an empty body); the post-scope MUST be the receiver scope unchanged because a class/module definition does not bind any local in its enclosing scope. The evaluator MUST push a new lexical class frame onto its `class_context` for the body's evaluation; nested `def`s consult the frame to resolve their RBS lookup. The frame's qualified name MUST be the rendered `constant_path` (e.g., `"Foo::Bar"` for `class Foo::Bar` and the join of every nested name for `class A; class B`).
- `Prism::SingletonClassNode` — same fresh-scope contract. When the singleton expression is `self`, the innermost lexical class frame MUST be flipped to `singleton: true` for the body's evaluation, so a `def foo` inside `class << self` resolves through `RbsLoader#singleton_method`. For non-`self` expressions the receiver class is not statically resolvable; the evaluator MUST keep the existing class context unchanged and accept that nested defs degrade to the `Dynamic[top]` parameter default.
- `Prism::LambdaNode` — the body is evaluated from the block-entry scope with the method's return sink suspended, as a nested `def` body is: `return` inside a lambda returns from the lambda, not from the enclosing method, so `cb = -> { return :skip }` MUST NOT add `:skip` to the method's inferred return type. The block of a `lambda { … }`, `define_method` or `define_singleton_method` call (implicit or `self` receiver, or through `send` / `public_send` / `__send__` with that name as a Symbol) MUST be evaluated the same way.
- `Prism::DefNode` — builds the method-entry scope by binding every named parameter through `Rigor::Inference::MethodParameterBinder` (see below) and evaluates the body under that scope. The pair's type MUST be `Constant[:method_name]` (matching Ruby's runtime behaviour of `def` evaluating to a Symbol) and the pair's scope MUST be the receiver scope unchanged (a `def` does not introduce a binding in its enclosing scope). The body MUST NOT see the outer scope's locals.

### Self typing (Slice A-engine)

`Rigor::Scope` carries an optional `self_type:` field, accessed through `Scope#self_type` and updated via `Scope#with_self_type(type)`. The field is `nil` for top-level scopes and is injected by the `StatementEvaluator` at the boundaries where Ruby gives `self` a definite identity:

- `Prism::ClassNode` / `Prism::ModuleNode` body: `self_type` MUST be `Singleton[<qualified-name>]` (because `self` inside the class body is the class object itself).
- `Prism::SingletonClassNode` body when the receiver is `self`: same as above (the body still runs with `self` = the enclosing class object), and the innermost class frame additionally flips to `singleton: true`.
- `Prism::DefNode` body: when the def is on the singleton side (`def self.foo` or any def lexically inside `class << self`) or the surrounding class frame is already singleton, `self_type` MUST be `Singleton[<qualified-name>]`. Otherwise (an ordinary instance def), `self_type` MUST be `Nominal[<qualified-name>]` with no type arguments. Top-level defs (no enclosing class) MUST leave `self_type` as `nil`.
- The block body of a class-creating meta call — `Class.new`, `Module.new`, `Struct.new`, `Data.define`, each with a literal block and a receiver that is the bare constant (or its `::`-rooted spelling) — is a class body: Ruby evaluates it with `self` bound to the class the call creates. `self_type` MUST be `Singleton[<anonymous-name>]` and the class context MUST carry one non-singleton frame under the same name, so a `def` in the body binds an instance method on that class. Unlike a `class` keyword body the block is a closure, so the outer scope's locals MUST stay visible. The `<anonymous-name>` is the per-call-site synthetic name defined below. At constant-write rvalue position (`Prism::ConstantWriteNode`, issue #590; `Prism::ConstantPathWriteNode` since [#703](https://github.com/rigortype/rigor/issues/703)) the same boundary holds with the constant's qualified name in place of the anonymous one — the key `ScopeIndexer` files the body's methods and member layout under (`meta_new_block_body`) — and the frame is appended to the enclosing class context rather than replacing it, so a body nested in `module M` is keyed `M::Const`. A PATH target MUST take the name a compact `class Holder::Const` header at the same position takes (`Source::ConstantPath.declaration_prefix`), ROOTED reset included: `::Holder::Const = Struct.new(…) do … end` written in `module Admin` names `Holder::Const`, so the frame stack MUST reset to it rather than gaining a frame under `Admin`. Declining the path spelling registered no factory at all, so the constant answered `singleton(Struct)` and its instance `Struct` — a class RBS knows — and every member read and block-defined override in it fired `call.undefined-method` on code Ruby runs. The walk MUST choose the key by the ScopeIndexer's own recognition, never a re-spelling of it: a rvalue that recognition does not key by the constant (a `Struct.new(*names)` whose members are not literal Symbols) was registered under the anonymous name and MUST be entered under it. A constant write whose rvalue is not a meta-new block form is typed through the expression typer with the scope unchanged, as before. The recognised WRITE spellings are `Const = …`, `Holder::Const = …` and their `||=` twins (`Prism::ConstantOrWriteNode` / `Prism::ConstantPathOrWriteNode`, since [#963](https://github.com/rigortype/rigor/issues/963)), because `Const ||= Struct.new(…) do … end` names its class exactly as the plain write does wherever the constant is unset — the case the define-once idiom is written for. `&&=` and the operator writes MUST NOT be recognised: neither names a freshly created class. Two value-preserving rvalue wrappers MUST be unwrapped before the recognition, in both this walk and every `ScopeIndexer` walk that keys a body by the constant: a `.freeze` tail (`Struct.new(:a) do … end.freeze` — `Module#freeze` answers its receiver, so the constant still holds the created class), repeated tails included, and a `Const = Const || <rvalue>` guard whose guarded name is the constant being written, the long spelling of `||=`. Only the argumentless, blockless, receiverful `.freeze` qualifies; `&.freeze` MUST NOT be unwrapped, its value being the receiver OR nil, and a guard over a DIFFERENT name (`A = B || Struct.new(:x)`) MUST NOT be unwrapped — `A` may hold `B`, whose class it does not name. The constant-publication census MUST thread the name a write gives the block's class THROUGH a `.freeze` tail: the tail sits between the write and the call whose block the constant names, so dropping the name there leaves the factory call opaque and suppresses every `self::X = …` the block publishes. It MUST keep declining the `||=` spellings, which it records unpublishable for the constant itself.

#### Anonymous class names

A class created by `Class.new do ... end` away from constant-write position has no constant to be keyed by, yet it owns whatever its block body defines. `Rigor::Inference::AnonymousMetaClass.name_for(call_node, source_path)` MUST supply one, spelled by `Rigor::Type::AnonymousClassName` as `#<Label:key>` where `Label` is the receiver constant (`Class`, `Module`, `Struct`, `Data`) and `key` is `source_path:line:column` (the path segment omitted when the caller has none). The spelling MUST NOT be producible by `Source::ConstantPath.qualified_name`, so a synthetic name can never collide with a real class in the discovery tables, and every pass over the same file MUST derive the same name from the same pair.

`ScopeIndexer` MUST register the block body's methods, def nodes and (for `Class.new(Parent)`) superclass under that name, and `MethodDispatcher`'s `Class.new` lift MUST answer `Singleton[<anonymous-name>]` for the parentless block form. For the `Struct.new` / `Data.define` block forms — anonymous and constant-keyed alike — it MUST also register every literal-Symbol member as a discovered `:instance` reader, exactly as it does for a `class X < Struct.new(...)` superclass, so a member read inside the body resolves as the struct's reader rather than falling through to a same-named `Kernel` private (`lambda` would otherwise read as a `Proc`). The parented form (`Class.new(Parent) do ... end`) MUST keep answering `Singleton[Parent]`, except that a block-free `Class.new` whose parent is a `Type::StructClass` or `Type::DataClass` MUST preserve that carrier so downstream construction retains its member layout. A block on that `Class.new` MUST decline the carrier lift because it can redefine a synthesised reader; constant-keyed forms recover safe folding through the reader-override tables. `ScopeIndexer` MAY unwrap nested, single-parent, block-free `Class.new(...)` calls to find a root `Struct.new(...)` / `Data.define(...)` layout, but MUST decline when an intermediate `Class.new` wrapper or the root factory call has its own block because those overrides belong to an intermediate superclass and cannot be keyed safely to the outer class. A `def` in an anonymous body MUST additionally remain in the `<toplevel>` def table (never clobbering a real top-level `def` of the same name): the body is a DSL block whose methods routinely reach some other object's `self`, and the leniency predates the name.

`Type::Singleton#describe` / `Type::Nominal#describe` MUST render an anonymous name without its key (`singleton(#<Class>)`), and `#erase_to_rbs` MUST answer `untyped` for one — the name is not valid RBS.

`ExpressionTyper` consumes the field in two places:

- `Prism::SelfNode` MUST type as `scope.self_type` when set, or `Dynamic[top]` when nil. This MUST NOT record a fallback event in either case.
- `Prism::CallNode` with `receiver: nil` (an implicit-self call such as `attr_reader_method`, `private_helper`, ...) MUST adopt `scope.self_type` as the receiver type for `MethodDispatcher.dispatch`. When `self_type` is nil, the receiver remains nil and the existing top-level fallback applies.

`Scope#==` and `Scope#hash` MUST include `self_type`. `Scope#join` MUST keep `self_type` when both sides agree and reset it to `nil` when they differ. `Scope#with_local`, `Scope#with_fact`, and `Scope#with_self_type` MUST all preserve the other two fields.

### Instance-, class-, and global-variable bindings (Slice 7 phase 1)

`Rigor::Scope` carries three additional binding maps alongside `locals`: `ivars`, `cvars`, and `globals` (each `Hash[Symbol, Type]`, default empty and frozen). The accessors are:

- `Scope#ivar(name)`, `#cvar(name)`, `#global(name)` — return the bound type for `name`, or `nil` when no write has been recorded in the analyzed slice of the program.
- `Scope#with_ivar(name, type)`, `#with_cvar`, `#with_global` — return a fresh scope with the named binding added. They MUST preserve `locals`, `fact_store`, `self_type`, and `declared_types` exactly like `with_local` does.

The `StatementEvaluator` MUST handle `Prism::InstanceVariableWriteNode`, `Prism::ClassVariableWriteNode`, and `Prism::GlobalVariableWriteNode` symmetrically with `LocalVariableWriteNode`: evaluate the rvalue under the entry scope, return `[rvalue_type, post_rvalue_scope.with_<kind>(name, rvalue_type)]`. The compound-write forms (`@x ||= …`, `@x &&= …`, `@x += …`) are bound in the post-scope by the Slice 7 phase 3 handlers specified below. The `ExpressionTyper` MUST handle `Prism::InstanceVariableReadNode`, `Prism::ClassVariableReadNode`, and `Prism::GlobalVariableReadNode` by consulting the corresponding scope binding map and falling through to `Dynamic[top]` (without recording a fallback event) when no binding exists.

`Scope#join` MUST union ivar/cvar/global bindings the same way it unions locals (drop any name not bound in BOTH sides; the statement-level evaluator is responsible for nil-injection on half-bound names if the catalogue ever extends to a control-flow construct that needs it). `Scope#==` and `Scope#hash` MUST include the three binding maps.

Slice 7 phase 2 lifts the method-local boundary for ivars: `Scope` carries a `class_ivars: Hash[String, Hash[Symbol, Type]]` accumulator keyed by qualified class name. `ScopeIndexer` populates it once at index time through a separate pre-pass that walks every `Prism::ClassNode` / `Prism::ModuleNode`, descends into each nested instance `Prism::DefNode`, and types every `Prism::InstanceVariableWriteNode` rvalue under a scope that carries the appropriate `self_type` (instance defs only — singleton defs operate on a different `self`). Multiple writes to the same ivar within the class union via `Type::Combinator.union`. `StatementEvaluator#build_method_entry_scope` MUST seed an instance method body's `ivars` from `Scope#class_ivars_for(current_class_path)`; singleton-method bodies (`def self.foo` or any def lexically inside `class << self`) MUST NOT consult the accumulator because their `self` is the class object itself.
