A class-level nominal (`Hash.new(0)`'s `Hash[Dynamic[top], Integer]`, `split`'s `Array[String]`) MUST be left as it is: a store of the same class keeps its claim, the unknown-store seam does not grow it either, and the arm would silence a genuine `COUNTS[k].upcase` and turn `rigor sig-gen`'s `-> Integer` into a skipped untyped return. The wrapper alone does not stop a stale read, because a call on a `Dynamic` receiver resolves on the static facet's RBS projection, where a closed shape answers its known values for any key, a tuple its known elements for any index and a value-pinned nominal its literal union: `H = { a: 1 }; H.default = 0` read `H[:b]` as `1` and folded `H[:b] == 0` always-falsey, `T = { a: 1 }; T[:a] = "x"` read `T[:a]` as `1` and drew an error-level `call.undefined-method` on `T[:a].upcase`, and `A = [1]; A << 2` folded `A.last == 2`. The open shape projects with a `Dynamic[top]` arm on both sides, so a direct read answers the known values beside the arm (`T[:a]` is `1 | Dynamic[top]`); the projection is not keyed, so those are every key's values, not the read key's own. A block parameter reads the same union (`PAIRS.each { |pair| }` over a mutated `[[:a, 1], [:b, 2]]` binds `Dynamic[top] | [:a, 1] | [:b, 2]`), and a destructured one (`|k, v|`) binds `Dynamic[top]`, because block auto-splat floors every position for the union's `Dynamic[top]` member. A constant or class variable no call mutates keeps its closed literal and every fold it licenses. The census sees only the declaring file's mutations, which is why a mutable literal publishes no value across files (§ Cross-file value constants).
- The Hash mutator table — `Inference::MutationWidening::HASH_MUTATORS` MUST list every Hash method that stores, removes or rewrites pairs in place, including each name it shares with `ARRAY_MUTATORS` (`shift`, `delete`, `delete_if`, `reject!`, `select!`, `filter!`, `keep_if`, `clear`, `compact!`, `replace`, `[]=`). The `HashShape` arm, the `non-empty-hash[K, V]` arm and the Hash side of the `Union` partition read only this table, so a name that reaches `SHAPE_MUTATORS` through the Array table alone is declined for every Hash carrier, and every consumer of the table (`UnknownStoreWidening`, `MutationRejoin`'s Hash arm, the ADR-56 block seam, the ADR-58 ivar census, `Effects::MutationClassifier` and the effect catalogue) misses the mutation with it. `k = { a: 1 }; k.shift` therefore types `Hash[Symbol, 1]`, as `k.delete(:a)` does, never the `{ a: 1 }` whose `k.size == 1` folds always-truthy on a hash that is empty at runtime; `s = { a: 1, b: 2 }; [1, 2].map { |i| v = s.size; s.shift; v }` types `[non-negative-int, non-negative-int]`, never `[2, 2]`.
- The Hash lookup mutators — `Hash#default=`, `Hash#default_proc=` and `Hash#compare_by_identity` leave the pair set alone and change what a read of it answers, so `Inference::HashLookupMutation::MUTATORS` lists them apart from `HASH_MUTATORS`, whose other readers (the `non-empty-hash` witness, `MutationRejoin`) take a listed name for a store or a removal. The effect classifier and the effect catalogue ask only whether the receiver was written, so they read the two tables as one, `Effects::MutationClassifier::HASH_MUTATORS` ([effect-summaries.md § Ownership](effect-summaries.md#ownership)). `MutationWidening.widen_for_mutator` MUST answer a `HashShape`, alone or as a `Union` member, under one of them through `HashLookupMutation.widen_shape`. `default=` / `default_proc=` MUST reopen a closed shape to `extra_keys: :open` with its pairs and key policy unchanged: a present key keeps its value, and a key outside `pairs` reads `untyped` by the open-shape rule of the shape tier, or the known values with a `Dynamic[top]` arm where the RBS projection answers, instead of the closed shape's `Constant[nil]`. A default proc may also store the key it is asked for (`proc { |h, k| h[k] = [] }`), which is what the open policy states. `compare_by_identity` MUST leave a shape whose every key is identity-stable (a Symbol, `true`, `false`, `nil`, an Integer in the fixnum range) unchanged, and MUST widen any other shape as a storing mutator does, value pins included, with a `Dynamic[top]` arm on the value side. A later `"k"` literal is the String the hash stored only when `# frozen_string_literal: true` interns both, so the read may hit, miss, or answer a default set before or after; the arm keeps it from folding `s["k"] == 1` or drawing `possible nil receiver` where the key does hit, and keeps a later `default=` from being answered by the known values. The widening MUST reach every place the engine recognises a mutator call on a variable: `SHAPE_MUTATORS` (so the block seam, the per-element fold and the block-return threading gate see it), an attribute compound write (`h.default ||= 0`) and a multi-assign attribute target (`h.default, x = 0, 1`), the ADR-58 ivar census (which widens a seed no method stores into through the same `widen_shape` rather than to its untyped floor) and `IndexedNarrowing`'s receiver-wide invalidation. A receiver the engine does not trace for any mutator — an alias (`g = h; g.default = 0`), a `tap` parameter, a hash element, `send(:default=, 0)` — is not reached. A content join MUST grow the seed as the body's lookup mutators on the same variable leave it: the slice-C block join reads its seed before `widen_after_block` runs, the `each_with_object` memo join reads the memo argument, and the `while` / `until` join reads the pre-body scope, so `b = { a: 1 }; [1].each { b.default = 0; b[:c] = 2 }` read `b[:zz]` as `1 | 2`, as did the same two statements in a `while` body. The constant census wraps any mutated constant in `Dynamic[…]` whatever the call, so it counts the three names as mutations too, and an all-Symbol constant loses its membership folds after `compare_by_identity`. `MutationWidening.widen_hash_shape` MUST widen an open shape to `Hash[Dynamic[top], Dynamic[top]]`, as it does an empty one, and `ShapeDispatch` MUST read an open shape's `default` as `untyped`, since the known values never bound a default: `{ a: 1 }` under `default = 0` and then `delete(:a)` read a missing key as `1`, and `h.default` as `1?`. `counts = { a: 1 }; counts.default = 0` therefore reads `counts[:b]` as `untyped` and `counts[:a]` as `1`, where `counts[:b] + 1` drew an error-level `call.undefined-method`; a hash with no default assignment still reads a missing key as `nil`. The reopened shape gives up the membership folds a closed shape makes (`size`, `keys`, `key?`), and a closed record parameter (`({ a: Integer }) -> void`) rejects it, as it rejects the nominal every other in-place mutation of a literal widens to; issue #1281 tracks that false positive.
- Issue #936 — the straight-line seam's empty-witness `Difference` arm, deferred by [ADR-56](../adr/56-block-captured-local-mutation.md) WD2.9. `Inference::MutationWidening.widen_for_mutator` MUST join a mutator's added content into a `non-empty-array[T]` / `non-empty-hash[K, V]` pre-state exactly as the `Tuple` / `HashShape` arms join theirs — through `join_added_elements` / `join_added_pairs`, over the refinement's BASE, with the same seed-admissibility test and the same one-store gradual floor — instead of answering the bare base. It MUST retract the empty witness only for the mutators that can leave the receiver empty: an Array mutator that only adds (`<<`, `push`, `append`, `prepend`, `unshift`, `concat`, `insert`) or only reorders or rewrites a fixed number of slots (`map!`, `collect!`, `sort!`, `sort_by!`, `reverse!`, `rotate!`, `shuffle!`) and a Hash mutator that only stores (`[]=`, `store`, `merge!`, `update`, `transform_keys!`, `transform_values!`) MUST keep it; every other name in the two tables retracts it, `replace` / `compact!` / `flatten!` / `uniq!` and `Hash#shift` included. Keeping the witness says nothing about the content: a rewriter among these takes the unknown-store entry's rewrite below and keeps the witness — `map!`, `collect!`, `transform_keys!` and `transform_values!` replace the position they rewrite, and `merge!` / `update` join `Dynamic[top]` beside the seed's arms. A widening that would answer the pre-state unchanged MUST decline instead: a reorder, an adder with no argument or whose join (gradual floor included) adds nothing the base does not already hold, or a rewriter whose positions already hold what it leaves there (`Dynamic[top]` alone under a total rewrite, any union carrying it under a partial one). `unless ys.empty?; ys.map!(&:to_sym)` therefore types `ys` as `non-empty-array[Dynamic[top]]`, never the `non-empty-array[String]` whose `ys.first.to_proc` drew `call.undefined-method`, while `ys.sort!` and `ys.reverse!` leave `non-empty-array[String]` exactly. `MutationWidening.joinable_receiver?` MUST accept such a `Difference` too, or the seam never types the arguments this arm now reads. `if xs.any?; xs << "s"` therefore types `xs` as `non-empty-array[… | String]`, never an `Array[…]` missing the appended arm, while `xs.clear` still answers the bare `Array[…]`.
- Issue #1140 — `Inference::ContentJoin.array_added_elements`'s `:[]=` arm MUST read the two splice forms through the stored value's splice contribution rather than contributing the value as one element: `arr[i, n] = other` (two PROVABLE index arguments — a syntactic `*splat` in an index position is passed to the join as `nil` and counts as arity-unknown since it may vanish at runtime, but an ordinary argument is provable however untyped it is) and `arr[range] = other` (every member of the index type a Range carrier — `Constant[Range]` or `Nominal["Range"]`, read through a `Difference` / `Refined` base) splice `other`'s elements into the receiver, so `a = []; a[0, 2] = [1, 2]` types `Array[Dynamic[top] | Integer]`, never `Array[Array[Integer] | Dynamic[top]]`. The splice contribution itself is member-wise: an Array member contributes its own element types (`Tuple` members, `Nominal["Array"]` type arguments, read through `Difference` / `Refined` bases — a bare `Nominal["Array"]` contributes `Dynamic[top]` alone because the signature left its elements unknown rather than absent), and a NON-Array member contributes ITSELF (`a[0, 1] = "x"` stores the String; Ruby inserts a non-Array RHS as one element, `nil` included — `a[i, n] = nil` stores a single nil, and assigning `[]` is the deletion form). Every member NOT proven an `Array` carrier MUST ALSO contribute `Dynamic[top]` for the elements a `to_ary` coercion would have spliced — `arr[i, n] = obj` invokes `obj.to_ary` when it exists, a `Nominal` member may be a subclass declaring one, and the project may reopen even a non-subclassable core class; this seam cannot prove the absence of `to_ary`, so the coercion arm is unconditional on non-Array members. An Array object is spliced directly — `Array#[]=` never invokes `to_ary` on it, subclass override or not — so `Tuple` and `Nominal["Array"]` members join exactly, and a member proven an `Array` subclass contributes `Dynamic[top]` alone for elements this seam cannot read. A scalar index (`arr[i] = v`) still contributes the value itself, and an index the engine cannot classify — a `Dynamic` member, a union mixing Range and non-Range members, a type broad enough to hold a `Range` at runtime (`Object`, `BasicObject`, `Enumerable`, `top` — anything `Nominal["Range"]` is passable to), or an argument list whose runtime arity a splat can shrink to one — MUST contribute BOTH readings, so the element union stays a superset of whichever form ran. Nominal `accepts` saying `no` in both directions is disjointness only for a member whose values are provably `Object` instances — a fixed-class value set that single inheritance keeps from being a Range; a resolved MODULE member (`Comparable`) or any member that fails that `Object` probe MUST read as broad, because `class MyRange < Range; include Comparable; end` satisfies the annotation and still splices at runtime. `IntegerRange` / `FloatRange` are scalar number refinements, not Range objects, and MUST answer as scalar indices. The classification is only as complete as the argument list it reads: every caller feeding an index-write node (`StatementEvaluator#index_write_arg_types` for the straight-line `a[0, 1] += v` / `a[i] ||= v` forms, `#index_write_block_arg_types` for the block-content scan) MUST pass ALL of the node's index arguments ahead of the stored value's type, because a two-index compound write whose second index is dropped classifies as a scalar store and the splice evidence never reaches the join. The block-path seam previously contributed nothing for index-writes at all; it MUST emit the same `[index_type..., stored]` shape, where `stored` is what the write stores through `[]=` — for a compound write the dispatched compound result (`a[i] op= v` stores the `receiver[i] op v` dispatch, `a[i] ||= v` / `a[i] &&= v` store `truthy(read) | v` / `falsey(read) | v`, the read typed as the `[]` dispatch on the receiver refined by a recorded indexed narrowing), never the rvalue alone; an `IndexTargetNode` — a multi-assign slot, a `for` index or a rescue reference — stays `Dynamic[top]` on this block path, which meets the target without the value its owning construct stores (the straight-line `eval_multi_write`, `for` index binding and rescue reference binding join that value, § Statement-Level Evaluation). The straight-line `eval_index_or_write` / `eval_index_write` handlers MUST type their expression result and joined evidence through the same compound result.
- Issue #643 — a content mutator whose RECEIVER is an element read (`c[0] << 5`, `b[0][0] << 1`, `xs.first << 3`, `ys.dig(0, 0) << 6`) mutates an rvalue temp, so no binding is named and the rules above record nothing. `Inference::ElementReadWidening.widen_element_read` MUST resolve a receiver built solely from `[]` (single argument), argument-less `first` / `last`, and `dig` reads rooted at a `Prism::LocalVariableReadNode` — a block on any hop, an arity that returns a sub-array (`first(2)`, a `[]` slice), or a root that is anything but a local MUST decline — then widen the element the path selects through `MutationWidening.widen_for_mutator` and write the rebuilt container back to that local. A literal Integer index selects that position (negative counting from the end, an out-of-range index selecting nothing and declining); a non-literal index MUST widen EVERY position, since which one was mutated is undecided. A `Union` at any hop maps memberwise, as in the rule above. The container's own arity MUST survive at every hop above the mutated element: mutating an element cannot change how many elements the container has. `c = [flag ? [1] : [2]]; c[0] << 5` therefore types `c` as `[Array[1 | Dynamic[top] | Integer] | Array[2 | Dynamic[top] | Integer]]`, so `c[0].last == 5` no longer folds always-falsey, and `b = [[[]]]; b[0][0] << 1; b[0][0].first` no longer answers `nil`. An unmutated container keeps its element pins whole, so `c = [[1]]; c[0].last == 1` still folds.
- Issue #580 — the straight-line widening is NOT a one-way door. `Inference::MutationWidening.widen_for_mutator` MUST accept a `Nominal[Array, [E]]` / `Nominal[Hash, [K, V]]` pre-state whose element / key / value parameter already carries a `Dynamic` arm, and re-join the mutator's added content into THAT parameter through the same `join_added_elements` / `join_added_pairs` the literal arms use; a parameter with no gradual arm MUST survive the re-join untouched, and a carrier with no gradual parameter at all MUST be declined outright. `MutationWidening.joinable_receiver?` MUST accept such a carrier too, or the seam never types the arguments the arm reads and the door closes itself. The gradual arm is the whole gate: growing a union that already carries `Dynamic[top]` cannot make it reject a declared type it accepted, manufacture a constant fold, or make a quiet dispatch fire, whereas growing a PRECISE `Array[:multi]` a hand-written signature declared is the #561 `def.return-type-mismatch` blast radius. The re-join's admissible-evidence seed MUST be the carrier's value-PINNED members only — the full parameter always contains the seam's own floor, which would switch the seed-admissibility gate off exactly where it guards, and a class set read off the previous join's own answer would floor every heterogeneous later store. A re-join MUST carry the per-slot `receiver[key] ||= default` narrowings keyed on the rebound name across the rebind: the binding still names the same object and only the summary of what it holds grew, so `Scope#with_local`'s ordinary drop (which assumes a DIFFERENT object) does not apply. Method-chain narrowings are not carried. `a = []; a.push(1); a.push("s")` therefore types `Array[Dynamic[top] | Integer | String]`, and `h = {}; h['a'] = {}; h['b'] = "s"; h['c'] = []` carries all three stores' value arms, while `temple = [:multi]; temple << [:static, "x"]` still answers `Array[:multi | Dynamic[top]]` and `Hash.new(0)`'s proved value parameter still reads `Integer` after `h[:x] += 1`.
- Unknown-store mutators — a mutator whose arguments do not describe what it stores. `Inference::RewriteMutation` lists them per carrier class with the type-argument positions each rewrites, in two tables. A TOTAL rewriter (`RewriteMutation::REPLACED`: `map!`, `collect!`, `flatten!` and `replace` an `Array`'s element, `replace` still joining its argument's elements afterwards; `transform_keys!` a `Hash`'s key, `transform_values!` its value, `replace` both) leaves nothing the seed held at the position, so the position MUST become `Dynamic[top]`; a PARTIAL one (`RewriteMutation::JOINED`: `fill` the element, `merge!` and `update` both `Hash` sides) may keep old values, so the position MUST join `Dynamic[top]` beside the seed's arms. `MutationWidening.join_added_elements` / `join_added_pairs` apply this first (`RewriteMutation.arm`, idempotent), so every path that reaches the content join takes it — the `Tuple` / `HashShape` arms, the #936 refinement arm, and the #580 re-join, where it MUST also apply after the precise parameters are restored, since a rewriter falsifies a proved side (`Hash.new(0)` under `transform_values!(&:to_s)`) where an adder's one store cannot; every other position keeps what the widening left. The value-pin erasure under a value-rewriting mutator MUST NOT run on a position either table lists (`RewriteMutation.values_mode`): it exists to stop a stale constant fold, which a union carrying `Dynamic` never produces, and the erased closed class beside a joined arm is what a hand-written signature pinning the seed rejects. A precise nominal stays declined (#580's gate): its element set is a declaration's claim, and RBS's own `map!` is `{ (Elem) -> Elem } -> self`, so `s = gets.to_s.split(","); s.map!(&:strip)` keeps `Array[String]`. `fill` is joined although its no-block form's argument is its value: the seam cannot see the block, and every straight-line store it joins is floored already. `merge!` on a counter opens the side `Hash.new(0)` proved (`Dynamic[top] | Integer`) for the same reason: the seam cannot tell a merging block or a foreign argument from neither. The ADR-56 slice-C join and the loop seam's content join (`StatementEvaluator#content_writeback_block_captures`, `#loop_content_writeback`) rebuild a collection from its seed and would drop the rewrite, so each MUST re-apply every rewriter the body calls on the local to its joined type (`StatementEvaluator#rewritten_capture`, through `RewriteMutation.arm_through`: member-wise over a `Union`'s nominal members, and over an empty-witness refinement's base, keeping the witness only when the mutator cannot empty the receiver) unless the seed is not a carrier the straight-line seam may grow (`MutationWidening.shape_carrier?`, asked of the seed as a whole, so a precise nominal inside a union with a growable member is rewritten too). A mixed `Array | Hash` seed reaches the rewrite with both carriers rederived (the #631 entry), so `x = gets ? [1] : { a: 1 }; [0].each { x.map!(&:to_s) if x.is_a?(Array); x[:k] = 2 if x.is_a?(Hash) }` types `Array[Dynamic[top]] | Hash[:k | Symbol, 1 | 2]`, where the kept `[1]` member drew `undefined method 'upcase' for 1`. The widening does not read the mutator's block, so the enumerator form (`d.map!.with_index { |x, i| x.to_s }`, `collect!.each_with_index`), which reaches it as the receiver of the enumerator call through the #1223 operand threading above, MUST take the same rewrite: the stored values are no more visible through the enumerator's block than through `map!`'s own. `a = [1]; a.map!(&:to_s)` therefore types `Array[Dynamic[top]]`, never the `Array[Integer]` whose `a.first.upcase` drew `call.undefined-method`; `a = [1, 2]; a.map!(&:to_s); a` satisfies `-> Array[String]`; `if ys.any?; ys.map!(&:to_sym)` types `non-empty-array[Dynamic[top]]`; `h = { a: 1 }; h.transform_keys!(&:to_s)` types `Hash[Dynamic[top], 1]`; `t = [:multi, :multi]; t.fill(:multi, 1)` answers `Array[:multi | Dynamic[top]]`, which haml's `-> Array[:multi]` accepts; and `a = [1]; [0].each { a.map!(&:to_s); a << "x" }` types `Array[Dynamic[top]]`, never `Array["x" | 1]`. The replacement costs one guard the kept seed would give: after a total rewrite the carrier holds no pinned member, so a later foreign store is admitted rather than floored, and `temple = [:multi]; temple.map!(&:itself); temple << [:static, x]` reads an `Array[…]` arm a hand-written `-> Array[:multi]` rejects.
- ADR-56 slice C — a store whose evidence reads a collection the join rederives. The block seam (`StatementEvaluator#content_writeback_block_captures`) and `each_with_object_return` type every store's evidence in the block-entry scope, where each content-mutated collection still holds its PRE-CALL contents, so a value computed from it (`h[k] = h[k] + 1`, `a << a.last + x`, `a[0] += 1`, `lens << buf.length` after `buf << w`) is the first iteration's answer, and the join closed over it. Both seams MUST join through `StatementEvaluator#join_content_to_fixpoint`, which MUST bind every name it joins to what that collection holds at any iteration's entry. The joined names are the block's captured content-mutated locals and, for `each_with_object_return`, the memo parameter as well; that seam MUST keep only the memo's carrier and leave the captured names' continuation to the block seam, so a memo store reading a captured collection the same block mutates (`buf << w; m << buf.length`) sees it past the first iteration too. A mutator's receiver is captured only when its `depth` exceeds the number of blocks and lambdas it is nested in within the block's body (`StatementEvaluator#captured_content_mutations`); `collect_content_mutations`' plain `depth >= 1` is that rule only directly in the body, and elsewhere takes a parameter of an inner block for the outer local it shadows. The memo's own stores MUST likewise be those whose receiver `depth` equals that nesting. A store nested in an inner block or lambda MUST have its evidence typed with every name those inner blocks introduce (`CapturedLocals.introduced_locals`) bound to `Dynamic[top]`: the seam's entry scope cannot see their bindings, and there the name resolves to the outer local it shadows (`|inner| picks << inner.first` inside the block must not read an outer `inner = [0]`). A name is MOVING when one of its stores reads a mutated Array or Hash — a `LocalVariableReadNode` of one among the mutator's arguments, a `[]=` call's stored value included — or is an `IndexOrWriteNode` / `IndexAndWriteNode` / `IndexOperatorWriteNode` on an Array-kind name (a mixed name's index writes are routed to the Hash side or floored, so none reads a slot it joins), whose stored value reads the slot it overwrites; every other name, and every String, is FIXED. A fixed name's evidence MUST be typed once, with each String name bound to `String` (its join, whatever it stored). The fixed name MUST then be bound to its own join plus the seed members that join refutes (`ContentJoin::NON_SURVIVING_CLASSES`, i.e. `nil`), which the first iteration's entry still holds. It MUST NOT be bound to the whole seed beside the join: the join already covers every other seed value, and re-adding a seed's literal shape widens dispatch (`a = []; [1].each { a[0, 1] ||= [2] }` must still read `Array[2]`). A moving name's evidence MUST be iterated to a fixpoint through `Inference::BodyFixpoint`, over that name's evidence slots only (an Array's element union, a Hash's key union and value union, all three for a mixed `Array | Hash` seed), each seeded `bot`. Each pass re-types the moving names' stores with every fixed name bound as above, and every moving name bound to its seed until its evidence is non-empty and thereafter, like a fixed name, to its join over the evidence so far plus the seed members that join refutes: a `nil` seed can still be `nil` on an iteration where another moving collection has already grown. The cap is 3; the moving evidence is value-pin widened (`Type::Combinator.widen_value_pinned`) on the final pass, and that widening is trusted without a further pass, as slice A's is; a slot that still moves floors to `Dynamic[top]`, its one-unknown-store answer, which joins beside the seed's own arms. Neither a seed nor a fixed name's evidence is ever widened. With no moving name the join is a single pass, so `acc = []; xs.each { |x| acc.push(x) }` still reads `Array[Integer]` with no gradual arm (#586). `h = { a: 0 }; [:a, :a, :a].each { |k| h[k] = h[k] + 1 }` therefore types `Hash[Symbol, 0 | Integer]`, never `Hash[:a | Symbol, 0 | 1]`, and `h[:a] == 3` no longer folds. The same block storing `1` keeps `Hash[:a | Symbol, 0 | 1]`, and `h[:a] == 3` still folds always-falsey there. `acc << 1` beside `sums << sums.last + x` keeps `Array[1]`. A collection the block changes only through a remover (`a.shift`) or only through an alias is not a joined name, so a store reading it still sees its pre-call contents. The join itself still drops a seed's `nil` after a guarded mutation (`x << v if x`) (#1219), and the final-pass widening is trusted without re-evaluating the body (#1220). A store that reads a local the body writes and the block-entry scope binds — an outer local the block rebinds (`LOCAL_WRITE_NODES` in its body or a parameter's default), or a block parameter or `;`-local it reassigns — MUST type that local as `Dynamic[top]` in its evidence (`StatementEvaluator#shadow_rebound_reads`, through the per-store `shadows` overlay). Both seams follow this rule. A joined collection the body rebinds is included, because the join's seed carries slice A's continuation. A local the body introduces already reads `Dynamic[top]`. A read or write counts only in the block's own scope or an outer one: its `depth` must reach past every inner block or lambda it is nested in, and a method, class or module body the block defines is a scope of its own. A read counts through a `LocalVariableReadNode` or a compound write that reads before it stores. An Array index write's index arguments, and every name they read, MUST keep the block-entry binding: the join classifies the store as an element or a splice from the index's type, and a `Dynamic` index reads as both, so `grid[i] = [x, x]` would join `x` beside the pair. A stored value that reads the same name keeps that binding too, so `ids[n] = n; n += 1` still stores `n`'s first-iteration value. A Hash key is covered. The block-entry scope binds such a local where the call found it, which is the first iteration's value, and no precise reading is sound yet. Slice A's continuation misses a value written between two rebinds, and joined with the block-entry typing it still stores exit values no store reads. A walk of the body to the store inherits the in-body flow's gaps (#1230, #1231, #1232, and #1223 until its fix), each of which then reports on correct code. `total = 0; out = []; [1, 2].each { |x| total += x; out << total }` therefore types `out` as `Array[Dynamic[top]]`, never `Array[0]`, and `out.last == 3` no longer folds. A store reading only locals the body does not write keeps its precise binding: beside `tally += x`, `caps << limit` still types `Array[5]`. A store reading an instance variable the body writes is not covered (#1235), and #1233 tracks a precise reading. The loop seam is outside this rule: it types its evidence against `post_loop`, where the straight-line seam has already given the binding its gradual arm.
- Issue #587 (b), per pair — `ExpressionTyper#try_hash_shape_block_fold` (`transform_values` / `transform_keys` and their bang forms over a closed `HashShape`) types the block once per pair, every pair from one entry scope, which is the same first-iteration pin as the per-element Tuple fold's. A `Prism::BlockNode` block MUST therefore lay the per-element fold's captured-local entry binding (`ExpressionTyper#per_element_captured_bindings`, the #587 (b) rule above) under every pair's parameter binding, computed once per call with the fixpoint's block parameter bound to the union of the shape's values for `transform_values` and of its `Constant` keys for `transform_keys`. It MUST be that binding, not a copy of it, so every rule of the #587 (b) entry — which names move, the suppression floor, the failure floor, the unmoved-pin floor, the in-place widening of a captured local the body mutates — holds per pair unchanged; issue #1198 is where the block-entry models consolidate. The instance variables the #587 (b) binding covers are part of it, so they move per pair too. A `&:symbol` block and an empty shape bind nothing. Unlike the Tuple fold, the per-pair fold carries no arity cap of its own, so #617's above-the-cap floor does not apply to it: every pair's body is threaded as for any block, and the binding holds at any pair count. `total = 0; { x: 1, y: 2 }.transform_values { total += 1 }` therefore types `{ x: Integer, y: Integer }`, never `{ x: 1, y: 1 }`, and nine pairs type `Integer` at every key, never their own values; `g = { a: 0 }; { x: 1, y: 2 }.transform_values do |e| g[:a] += 1; g[:a] end` types `{ x: Dynamic[top] | Integer, y: Dynamic[top] | Integer }`, never `{ x: 0, y: 0 }`; a body that rebinds `total` but answers an untouched `k = 5` as its tail (`total += e; k`) still folds to `{ x: 5, y: 5 }`; and a `transform_keys` body whose new key the rebound local spells (`i += 1; k.to_s + i.to_s`) declines to the dispatcher instead of folding `{ "a1": 1, "b1": 2 }`, and answers `Hash[String, 1 | 2]` there through the generic pass's own binding (the next entry). Running nested inside a body another pass evaluates whole is the one way the per-pair fold reaches a tail-only pair #617's dependency predicate can flag, and there the fold MUST take #617's tail-only floor on the Tuple fold's terms, per pair and over the same binding, and MUST answer it rather than decline — a decline reaches the dispatcher, whose block-return pass the same suppression types tail-only. `transform_values` keeps its keys and answers every value `Dynamic[top]`; `transform_keys` answers `Hash[Dynamic[top], union(<the shape's values>)]`, since a `Dynamic[top]` key cannot index a `HashShape` and two new keys may collide. An empty shape types no pair and keeps its exact `{}`. For the same reason a `transform_keys` fold that runs under the suppression and cannot build a `HashShape` — a new key that is no Symbol / String `Constant`, or a collision — MUST answer `Hash[union(<the new key types>), union(<the shape's values>)]` from the pairs it typed over the #587 (b) binding, or that floor when a pair could not be typed, rather than decline: `buf = +"k"; … { a: 1 }.transform_keys { |k| buf << w.to_s; buf }` types `Hash[String, 1]`, never the dispatcher's tail-only `Hash["k", 1]`. Outside the suppression it still declines. `{ x: [], y: [] }.transform_values do |a| a << w; a end` inside a threaded `m.synchronize do w = v; … end` therefore types `{ x: Dynamic[top], y: Dynamic[top] }`, never `{ x: [], y: [] }`, whose values each read `first` as a provable `nil`, and `{ a: 1, b: 2 }.transform_keys do |k| k = "#{k}#{w}"; k end` there types `Hash[Dynamic[top], 1 | 2]`, never the entry `{ a: 1, b: 2 }`.
- HashShape per-pair fold, arguments and in-place forms — `ExpressionTyper#try_hash_shape_block_fold` types each pair's block from the call's entry scope and assembles pairs in iteration order. Where Ruby's iteration does not match that model, it MUST decline to the dispatcher's `Hash` nominal in cases (1) and (2), and MUST reorder the pairs in case (3). (1) A call with any argument. `transform_keys(mapping)` renames every key that `mapping` names without yielding it, and the fold never reads `mapping`; `transform_values` takes no argument. `r = { a: 1, b: 2 }.transform_keys({ a: :z }) { |k| k }` is `{ z: 1, b: 2 }` at runtime, the fold answered `{ a: 1, b: 2 }`, and `r[:z] + 1` then reported a `nil` receiver on correct code. For the forms it accepts, the declined call's `Hash` nominal comes from the mapping-form rule under generic dispatch (`HashTransformKeysFolding`). (2) A bang form (`transform_values!` / `transform_keys!`) whose `Prism::BlockNode` block is not receiver-blind (`Inference::ReceiverBlindBlock.blind?`). The bang forms rewrite the receiver pair by pair while they iterate, so a block that can see the receiver sees pairs it has already rewritten: `h = { x: 1, y: 2 }; h.transform_values! { |e| h[:x] + e }` is `{ x: 2, y: 4 }` at runtime, never `{ x: 2, y: 3 }`. The engine records no aliasing between bindings, and a method can return the receiver under any name (`other = h`, a constant naming it, `h.method(:[])`, `Registry.store` for `def self.store = STORE`), so the gate MUST NOT look for the receiver. A block is receiver-blind when every node the walk reaches, at any depth, is admitted. The operand of `defined?` is not walked, because it reads no value. A local declared inside the block tree is admitted; Prism's `depth` decides this, so a nested parameter that shadows an outer local is still a parameter. A captured local, instance, class or global variable, or a constant, is admitted when its entry type carries no hash contents, and a compound write is checked as the read it performs first. A constant compound write (`F ||= v`) names no read node to type, and a constant path under a non-constant parent (`m::T`) cannot be typed from the entry scope, so neither is admitted. A method call is admitted when its receiver is built from block parameters and literals alone, interpolated strings and symbols included. That excludes an implicit-self call and a call on `self`, a constant, a captured variable or a block-local. `super`, `yield` and a nested `def` / `class` / `module` / `class << x` are never admitted; any other node is. A type carries hash contents when it is a `HashShape` or a `Hash` nominal with a parameter other than `Dynamic` or `top`, or when it holds one as a `Union` / `Intersection` member, `Tuple` element, `Nominal` type argument, `Refined` / `Difference` base, `StructInstance` / `DataInstance` member, `Dynamic` static facet, `BoundMethod` receiver, `Maybe` / `Result` component, or `App` argument or bound. A captured scalar therefore keeps the fold (`h.transform_values! { |e| e * factor }` folds `{ x: 3, y: 6 }`), and so do a `&:symbol` block and `[e].map { |h| h + 1 }.first`. The non-bang forms build a new hash and leave the receiver alone, so they MUST keep the exact fold: `h.transform_values { |e| h[:x] + e }` stays `{ x: 2, y: 3 }`. What a method called on a block parameter reaches is outside the gate; that is the engine-wide method-effect gap. The gate costs precision only on the value of the bang call itself, because `MutationWidening` already widens the binding a bang call is made on. (3) A folded `transform_keys!` MUST order its pairs the way CRuby's in-place rewrite leaves them, not in iteration order. For each old pair, `rb_hash_transform_keys_bang` deletes the old key unless an earlier pair already produced it as a new key, then stores the new key: in place when that key is an old key not yet reached, appended otherwise. `{ a: 1, b: 2, c: 3 }.transform_keys! { |k| k == :b ? :c : (k == :c ? :b : k) }` therefore types `{ c: 2, a: 1, b: 3 }`, while the non-bang form builds `{ a: 1, c: 2, b: 3 }`. The iteration order had made `r.keys.first == :a` fold always truthy on correct code.
- Per-element Tuple fold, arguments — `ExpressionTyper#try_per_element_block_fold` reads only the block, so it MUST decline to the dispatcher on a call with any argument. `index(value)` / `find_index(value)` search by `==` without running the block, and `find(ifnone)` / `detect(ifnone)` answer `ifnone.call` when no position matches; the fold's other methods take no argument. `r = [1, 2].find(-> { 0 }) { |e| e > 5 }` is `0` at runtime, the fold answered `nil`, and `r + 1` then reported a `nil` receiver on correct code. `BlockFolding`'s falsey-block `nil` short-circuit declines an argument too, so the call takes its RBS answer: `[1, 2].find(-> { 0 }) { … }` answers `find`'s `(Elem | T)` projection, `1 | 2 | Dynamic[top]`. `detect(ifnone)` shares that projection on every receiver whose `detect` is Enumerable's or Array's: rbs 4.1 and later alias `Array#detect` to `find`, and the `Enumerable#detect` ifnone overlay below restores the fallback arm upstream omits (for `Array` too, before rbs 4.1). A block-only `find` keeps its exact fold.
- Constant-block filter endpoints, result class — `MethodDispatcher::BlockFolding#fold_filter` answers a `select` / `filter` / `reject` / `take_while` / `drop_while` call whose block is a `Constant` with one of two endpoints, and each endpoint MUST be a value of the class the METHOD returns, not of the receiver's class. `Hash#select` / `#filter` / `#reject` return a Hash: the all-dropped endpoint over a Hash receiver (a `HashShape`, a `Hash` nominal, or a `non-empty-hash` `Difference`) is the empty `HashShape`, and the all-kept endpoint is the receiver. Every other combination is Enumerable's and returns an Array — `take_while` / `drop_while` on a Hash, and all five on a `Set` or `Range` (`Set` defines only the bang forms) — so its all-dropped endpoint is the empty-tuple carrier and its all-kept endpoint MUST decline to the RBS tier's `Array[Elem]` projection rather than answer the receiver. The fold once answered `[]` for every all-dropped call, so `r = { a: :q }.reject { |_k, _v| true }; r[:x] = 1` reported `call.argument-type-mismatch` on `Array#[]=`, and answered the receiver for every all-kept call, so `Set[1, 2].select { true }` typed as a `Set` and `{ a: :q }.take_while { true }` as a Hash. An Array receiver keeps both endpoints unchanged.
- Constant-block predicate folds, pattern argument — `MethodDispatcher::BlockFolding#fold_predicate` answers an `all?` / `any?` / `none?` call from the block's `Constant` truthiness and the receiver's emptiness, so it MUST decline a call with any argument. Given a pattern, Ruby tests `pattern === element` and ignores the block (warning "given block not used"): `[1, 2].all?(String) { true }` is `false` and `[1, 2].any?(Integer) { false }` is `true`, but the fold answered the block's side, and a condition on the result then read as always truthy or always falsey on correct code. The declined call takes the RBS `bool`, as `count` and the `find` family's falsey-block `nil` short-circuit already do for an argument.
- Non-empty `min_by` / `max_by` — `MethodDispatcher::BlockFolding#fold_edge_by` answers a `min_by` / `max_by` call that passes a block and no argument, and it MUST answer the receiver's element type without `nil` when the receiver is a non-empty `Tuple` (the union of its elements) or a non-empty constant integer `Range` (its element range, `Integer[1..3]` for `(1..3)` or `Range.new(1, 3)`). Ruby answers `nil` only for an empty receiver, and the block's value picks which element rather than whether one is returned, so the rule reads no block truthiness; the RBS `Elem?` return made `m = [1, 2].min_by { |s| rand(3) }; m + 1` report `call.possible-nil-receiver` on correct code. It MUST decline an empty receiver, a receiver whose size is not static (`Array[T]`, an endless or non-integer `Range`), and the count form `min_by(n) { … }`, whose answer is an Array; each takes its RBS answer. The answer is the whole element union, never one element: the one `block_type` the dispatcher holds is typed from the call's entry scope and cannot say which position wins.
- Issue #587 (b), the generic block-return pass — `ExpressionTyper#block_entry_scope`, which types a block's value for `MethodDispatcher.dispatch` (and the #853 `break` arms) whenever no fold answers, types the body once from the call's entry scope. That is the same first-iteration pin: over a nominal receiver `seen = 0; xs.all? { |x| seen += 1; seen == 1 }` read `seen` as `0` and folded to `true`, `xs.find { |x| seen += 1; seen == 2 }` folded to `nil` and fired always-falsey, `xs.map { total += 1; total }` typed `Array[1]`, and a `transform_keys` whose per-pair fold declined answered only the keys the entry binding spelled (`flag = true; { a: 1, b: 2 }.transform_keys { |k| out = flag ? k : :other; flag = false; out }` typed `Hash[:a | :b, 1 | 2]`). When the call may run its block more than once (`ExpressionTyper#block_may_repeat?`), the pass MUST lay the #587 (b) binding (`ExpressionTyper#captured_block_bindings`, the rule above in full: the name set, the in-place widening, the scan and both floors, the suppression and failure floors, the marks) under the block's parameter bindings, computed with the fixpoint's block parameters bound to the signature's expected parameter types; the #853 `break`-arm scan MUST lay it on the same condition. The value pass MUST NOT lay it under an iterator that discards its block's value (`each` and its `each_*` / `reverse_each` / `each_with_object` siblings, `times`, `upto`, `downto`, `step`): the block's type there only selects the block-bearing overload, so the fixpoint would buy a type nothing reads, on top of the ADR-56 write-back's own passes. A call `ClosureEscapeAnalyzer` classifies `:non_escaping` is the evidence of repetition, and so is a call it classifies `:unknown` whose method name some catalogue entry lists as iteration (`ClosureEscapeAnalyzer.iterator_name?`: every name the class catalogue lists, less `tap` / `then` / `yield_self`; issue [#1234](https://github.com/rigortype/rigor/issues/1234)). An unclassified receiver — `Dynamic[top]`, a union, a project class whose ancestry the analyzer cannot follow — kept the entry scope before this, so `items.all? { |x| seen += 1; seen == 1 }` on an untyped `items` folded to `true` and fired always-truthy on its result. The name reading belongs to this pass alone: escape analysis MUST keep treating the same `:unknown` call as unproven. Two kinds of repeating call MUST still be excluded: `tap` / `then` / `yield_self`, catalogued there too, run their block exactly once (`BlockCallTiming`), and a receiver that provably holds at most one element — a `Tuple` of at most one element, a closed `HashShape` of at most one pair, a constant `Integer` of at most 1 under `times`, a constant integer `Range` of at most one element — runs it at most once however it iterates (`done = false; [:only].each { break if done; done = true }` is `[:only]`, and the cross-iteration `done` would type it `Array[Symbol]?`). Every other call MUST keep the plain entry scope: it is exact for a block run once, where a cross-iteration binding would add values no read ever sees — `first = true; w = 5.then { |n| was = first; first = false; was ? n : nil }` would type `w` as `Integer?` and fire `call.possible-nil-receiver` on `w + 1`, and `m.synchronize { out = buf; buf = nil; out }` would add `nil` to the taken value — and for an iterator whose name the catalogue does not list it remains the first-iteration pin. A call classified `:escaping` keeps the entry scope too. A failure computing the binding MUST fall back to the plain entry scope rather than take out the pass. A body that rebinds and mutates nothing captured binds nothing. So `xs.map { total += 1; total }` types `Array[Integer]`, the `all?` and `find` above no longer fold, the `transform_keys` types `Hash[:a | :b | :other, 1 | 2]`, and `xs.all? { |x| k = 1; k == 1 }`, whose only rebind is block-local, still folds to `true`.
- Block jump paths — one block invocation ends at its fall-through AND at every `next` that targets the block; a `break` that targets it ends the whole call. The jumps that target a construct are the ones reachable from its body without crossing a nested block, lambda, `def`, loop, or class body (`Inference::JumpTargets`, the one scan `ExpressionTyper`'s block-return passes and `StatementEvaluator`'s loop and block joins share). `StatementEvaluator#evaluate_invocation` MUST answer, as the invocation's exit scope, the body's fall-through scope joined (`Scope#join`) with the scope at each block-level `next`, and `eval_block` MUST return that exit scope. The `next` scopes are collected into a sink threaded through `sub_eval` rather than a thread-local, so an evaluation `ExpressionTyper` starts elsewhere cannot contribute one; a `next` that belongs to a nested construct MUST NOT join, and a body with no block-level `next` MUST keep its fall-through scope unchanged. A jump's scope is read after its arguments are evaluated, so `next(n = :odd)` leaves with its write; that evaluation MUST NOT record into the per-node scope index, since whether a sink is collecting depends on unrelated context. A jump inside `begin … ensure` leaves only after the `ensure` clause has run, so `eval_begin` MUST carry every jump scope its clauses recorded through that clause (again without recording) before a consumer reads it: `buf = nil; next if c` under `ensure buf = +"reset"` leaves with `"reset"`. Both readers of the exit scope take the join: [ADR-56](../adr/56-block-captured-local-mutation.md)'s write-back fixpoint (the sub-phase 3b entry above) and the #587 (b) per-element / per-pair fixpoint, which MUST evaluate through `evaluate_invocation`. The #617 unmoved-pin floor of that fixpoint MUST tell the paths apart, because a rebind on a `next` arm moves the joined binding off its seed while an unthreaded write on the fall-through (`(seen += 1) == 2`) stays invisible: a name some pass's FALL-THROUGH moved off that pass's entry is believed, a name only a `next` arm moved MUST count as unmoved and take the floor when its call-site binding is value-pinned, and a name neither moved is judged against its seed exactly as a block without a block-level `next` always was. A block-level `break` MUST NOT join the invocation's exit scope: its binding starts no further iteration, and fed back into the fixpoint it would type the next pass under a value that pass never sees. The ADR-56 write-back MUST instead union each rebound name's binding at every block-level `break` into the converged continuation binding, reading the `break` scopes of a pass whose entry is the converged binding: a fixpoint that stabilised reuses its own last pass, and only a capped fixpoint — whose widened binding no pass ran from — evaluates the body once more, without recording into the per-node scope index, which keeps the fixpoint's own last pass for the check path's diagnostics. A name the fixpoint floored to `Dynamic[top]` MUST keep the floor. A `while` / `until` / `for` body collects its own `next` scopes through the same sink and scan, and its `break` scopes through the loop break sink, which records the same post-argument, post-`ensure` scope (the `Prism::WhileNode` and `Prism::ForNode` bullets above); a nested loop's `next` therefore never reaches a block's sink, while a `->` body, evaluated under the enclosing collection, still does and is dropped by node identity. A jump inside a call's receiver or arguments, a literal, an interpolation or a `rescue` modifier is reached through the operand threading of the `Prism::CallNode` entry above and joins like any other (`puts((g = e).odd? && next)`). A jump in a position the walk still types as a pure value (a `when` condition, a `yield` or `super` argument) is not collected, the same limit the walk has for a plain write there. `n = String.new; [1, 2, 3].each { |e| if e.odd?; n = e; next; end }` therefore types `n` as `1 | 2 | 3 | String`, never `String`; `found = nil; [1, 2, 3].each { |x| if x > 1; found = x; break; end }` types `found` as `2 | 3 | nil`, never `nil`, while the body's own read of a local only a `break` rebinds still sees its entry binding; and a body that rebinds nothing on a jump path keeps its exact binding.

- Issue #617 — the three remaining ways a block-return answer could still be the pre-state rather than the value, and the straight-line carrier the mutation seam did not respond to.
  - **A compound write as an expression.** `ExpressionTyper` MUST type `v += x` / `@v ||= x` / `$v &&= x` as the value it STORES — `union(narrow_truthy(current), rhs)` for `||=`, `union(narrow_falsey(current), rhs)` for `&&=`, and the operator dispatched on the current binding otherwise — never as `rhs` alone when the target is BOUND. An UNBOUND `||=` target MUST read as `rhs`: nothing wrote the variable on any path the analyzer saw, which is the memoization idiom (`@default ||= new`), and the ADR-5 optimistic reading is what lets `sig-gen` type every memoized singleton; an unbound operator-write target reads `Dynamic[top]`. The reading needs an rvalue with a truthy part. An unbound `||=` whose rvalue has none is a guard, not a memo (`@settings ||= raise "boot first"`, `@verbose ||= false`): it returns only when something the analyzer did not see set the target, so it MUST read `union(narrow_truthy(Dynamic[top]), rhs)` — `Dynamic[top]`, `Dynamic[top] | false` — which is the statement evaluator's reading of any unbound target, never the rvalue alone. Typed as the rvalue, `def self.settings = (@settings ||= raise "boot first")` beside a `configure` that stores the settings made `sig-gen` declare `def self.settings: () -> bot`. A local takes the exception too, although nothing unseen writes one: the scope a loop body is typed from does not carry the previous iteration's write, so a `while` body that reads `v ||= raise "unset"` behind an `i > 0` test before its `v = 1` typed the write `bot` where Ruby answers `1`. An instance method reads its ivar through the class-wide seed (ADR-58), so that seed MUST NOT take a contribution from a `||=` whose rvalue has no truthy part (`ScopeIndexer#record_ivar_or_write`): the write stores nothing truthy, and seeded as `union(bot, nil)`, which is `nil`, the guard bound its own ivar and read `truthy(nil) | bot`, which is `bot`, again — `def settings = (@settings ||= raise NotImplementedError)` in a concern whose including class stores the settings. The exception costs a target nothing writes: a straight-line local no earlier statement assigns, or an ivar no method stores, is `nil` at runtime, so `(q ||= nil).upcase` and `(@n ||= nil) + 1` no longer report the call on `nil`. The statement form (`s = (q ||= nil)`) already read `Dynamic[top]?`, and the index rule takes the same trade. The memo reading keeps the loop-scope limit: the same `while` reads `u ||= 2` ahead of `u = 1` as `2` where Ruby answers `1`. An unbound `&&=` target is no memo, as `h[k] &&= v` on an absent slot is not: an unset `@x &&= v` returns `nil` without evaluating `v` (an unset constant or class variable raises), so besides `rhs` its value is the target's own falsey value — `nil` when nothing set it, or a falsey value a write the analyzer did not see stored — and it MUST read `union(narrow_falsey(Dynamic[top]), rhs)`, which the engine carries as `Dynamic[top] | rhs`: the statement evaluator's reading, whatever the rvalue. The gradual reading gives up one precise answer: `def close = (@io &&= nil)` on an ivar nothing else writes is `nil` at runtime, but no longer reads as `nil`, so `sig-gen` skips it rather than declaring `-> nil`. Read as `rhs`, `if (@x &&= 1)` folded always-truthy on a program that takes the else arm, and `@x &&= raise("b")` typed `bot` where the statement form read `Dynamic[top]`. The class-wide ivar seed MUST NOT bind an ivar that only `&&=` writes, since the write cannot give an ivar its first value (`ScopeIndexer#record_ivar_and_write`): an `&&=` contribution counts only beside another write of the ivar, in either source order, and an ivar with none stays unseeded, so an instance method's `&&=` takes the unbound reading too. An `op=` contribution (`ScopeIndexer#record_ivar_operator_write`) MUST NOT depend on source order either, since Ruby may call the class's methods in any order ([#1343](https://github.com/rigortype/rigor/pull/1343)). It MUST be dispatched on the ivar's complete seed, `&&=` contributions included, and never on the seed as the pre-pass has walked it so far: a `+=` written above `@x &&= 1.5` dropped `Float` from the seed and folded `x == 2.5` always-falsey. An ivar that only `op=` writes is seeded from the widened rvalues first, and that seed counts as a write for `&&=`. When the dispatcher declines the seed as a whole because some member cannot take the operator (`nil + 1` beside a `reset` or a `||=`), the members it can type MUST still be dispatched, together: declined whole, `@x = 0.5; @x += 1` beside `@x = nil` lost `Float` and folded `l > 1.0` under `l.is_a?(Float)` always-falsey. A declining literal, or a nominal whose class the environment knows, lacks the operator as far as the RBS says, so its write raises: it MUST add nothing, since the rvalue joined for it put an `Integer` no run can store beside `0.5 | Float | nil` and fired `def.return-type-mismatch` on a `-> Float?` reader. Any other declining member (a `Dynamic`, a class only source defines) keeps the rvalue as its stand-in, and a seed with no typed member falls back to the rvalue. The RBS reading misses a source monkey patch of a core operator, an operator the RBS omits, and the `nil` an ivar holds before any write, which the seed does not model; each reads as raising. Because one write's result is another's receiver, the dispatch MUST repeat once per held write of the ivar, which covers every chain one call of each method can form. A chain that would continue from a receiver (the seed widened off its value-pinned members) wider than 40 members MUST floor to `Dynamic[top]`; that is a cost guard local to this chain, not ADR-41's `union_size` budget, which stays unwired. It MUST NOT go on to a fixed point: re-dispatched on its own `Dynamic[Integer | …]` result, a lone counter's `@n += x` gained `Dynamic[top]`, and the widening pass that forces convergence dropped the literal the counter starts at. This is `StatementEvaluator#compound_result_type`'s algebra, and the two MUST agree: the expression form is what a block body typed WITHOUT the evaluator reads, so `total = 0; [1, 2].map { total += 1 }` typed `[1, 1]` and fired a `flow.always-truthy-condition` on `r.last == 1`, which is `2` at runtime. A constant target (`ConstantOrWriteNode` / `ConstantAndWriteNode` / `ConstantOperatorWriteNode` and their `ConstantPath*WriteNode` forms, `ExpressionTyper#type_of_compound_constant_write`) MUST take the same algebra. Its current binding is what a plain read of the same spelling resolves to at the write site (`Reflection.resolve_constant_type`: the lexical ladder, the in-source table of plain `=` writes, then RBS), less what a recorded top-level body takes from its caller (`caller_derived: false`) — the caller's namespaces and ancestors, for a bare name and for a path's head. An unset top-level `REGISTRY ||= {}` is the memo, and a caller's `Plugin::REGISTRY` resolved there typed `def registry = (REGISTRY ||= {})` as `"plugins" | {}` inside `Plugin`. A path's later segments still walk their owner's ancestors, as Ruby does, so a top-level `Client::DEFAULTS ||= []` still finds `Base::DEFAULTS` through `Client < Base`. An unresolved constant, and a path whose base renders no static name (`klass::X`, `self::X`), is UNBOUND and reads as above, so the memoizing `REGISTRY ||= {}` — legal in a `def` body, where a plain `REGISTRY = {}` is a dynamic constant assignment — keeps its rvalue. The guard exception applies as it does to a variable: an unbound `SETTINGS ||= raise "boot first"` reads `Dynamic[top]`, not the rvalue's `bot`. A constant the census (§ Cross-file value constants) sees bound MUST NOT read as unbound when that plain read resolves nothing. Another file's unpublishable value (`H2 = { x: 1 }`) never publishes, a publishable one is withdrawn by the compound write itself, which the census counts as a second writer, and this file's own multi-assign or chained write (`A, B = …`, `A = B = …`) is not in its table; each binds a value the analyzer does not carry. The binding MUST be `Dynamic[top]`, and `||=` / `&&=` then read `Dynamic[top] | rhs`: beside a sibling `H2 = { x: 1 }`, `w = (H2 ||= 0); w[:x]` reported `Integer#[]`. Any write other than a `||=` binds, a sibling operator write or `&&=` in the same file included (`def count = COUNT ||= 0` beside `def bump = COUNT += 1` reads `0 | Dynamic[top]`). A name the census records as written only through `||=` (the `CONSTANT_MEMO` descriptor, per file) MUST NOT bind while those memos, across every census name with its last segment, sit in one file: that is the memoization idiom, so `REGISTRY ||= {}` keeps its memo reading however often its file repeats it, and at a call site whose callee body the caller's scope types. Memos of the segment in two or more files MUST bind, because either may load first and set the constant the other then reads: `DEFAULTS ||= { timeout: 5 }` in one file made `d = (DEFAULTS ||= {}); d[:timeout] + 1` in another report `+` on `nil`. Files MUST be counted by expanded path, since discovery can walk one file under two spellings (`lib/b.rb`, `./lib/b.rb`); a path is not resolved through symlinks, so a symlinked working directory can still count one file twice, which only widens. Counting by segment rather than by census name over-binds two unrelated memos (`A::CACHE`, `B::CACHE` in two files read `Dynamic[top] | rhs`), which only widens too. Memos of one name repeated in ONE file keep the idiom's reading even when their rvalues differ: `def a = (X4 ||= { x: 1 })` beside `w = (X4 ||= 0); w[:x]` in another method still reports `Integer#[]`, the same flow-insensitivity limit the table has below. A `self::X ||=` / `klass::X ||=` never reaches the resolver that records the `constant:<segment>` edge for every other form, so it MUST record that edge itself: its answer depends on another file's write of the segment. Which name the write reads MUST be decided by the same resolution run again with only the binding names in its in-source table (`Scope#bound_constant_names`, grouped by last segment), never by the spelling's last segment alone, so a sibling `Other::REGISTRY = []` leaves a top-level `REGISTRY ||= {}` its memo reading, and the rerun keeps the rooted marker and `caller_derived: false`. Two census spellings bind without the rerun, because the ladder never tries them: a wildcard for the segment (`k::SLOT = 1`), which may have written any of them, as the conflict rule reads it; and a path the census keeps as written ([#690](https://github.com/rigortype/rigor/issues/690)) when the write's spelling ends with it, so `Foo::BAR = …` inside `module M` binds `M::Foo::BAR ||= 0`. A path whose base renders no static name (`self::X ||= v`, `klass::X ||= v`) binds when any binding name shares its last segment. The rerun happens only when the plain read resolves nothing. A shadowing name the plain read tries above a lower rung already stops it (§ [A written candidate stops the ladder](#a-written-candidate-stops-the-ladder)), so another file's unpublished `App::LIMIT` makes `LIMIT ||= 1` inside `App` read `1 | Dynamic[top]` where it folded the published top-level `LIMIT`, and a plain read of `LIMIT` there reads `Dynamic[top]` too. The binding names come from the project discovery a whole run seeds; a run whose discovery leaves out the writing file (the in-memory single-source `Runner#run_source`, editor mode over a target set without that file, a `prebuilt:` runner) keeps the unbound reading, and a write inside `class <<`, which the census does not name, binds nothing. One limit follows from the table the binding is read from: it is flow-insensitive and holds only plain writes, so a value a compound write stored is not in the binding: `NAME = nil; NAME ||= "bob"; NAME &&= "x"` reads `"x"?` where Ruby answers `"x"`, a reassignment Ruby itself warns about. Typed as the rvalue alone, `H = { x: 1 }; v = (H ||= 0); v[:x]` reported `Integer#[]` on a program whose `v` is `H`, and `F.transform_values { |e| F ||= 0 }` folded every value to `0`. Index targets follow the rule after this list.
  - **The `find` / `detect` / `find_index` / `index` family under the per-element fold.** A position whose predicate folds truthy after only undecided and falsey ones ends the search whatever the undecided ones answer, so the assembler MUST answer then: `find` / `detect` the union of that position's element and every undecided earlier one's, `find_index` / `index` the union of their indices, with no `nil`. When the fold has typed every position and the assembler still declines — which happens only because some position's predicate is not a `Constant` and none after it folds truthy — the fold MUST answer a floor rather than fall through to the dispatcher: `union(<receiver elements>, nil)` for `find` / `detect`, `Integer?` for `find_index` / `index`. Falling through hands the answer to `BlockFolding`, which reads ONE block-return type computed from the call's ENTRY scope and short-circuits the whole call to `nil` on a provably-falsey block, so `seen = 0; [1, 2].find do |e| seen += 1; seen == 2 end` answered `nil` where Ruby answers `2`. The filter family (`select` / `filter` / `reject`) MUST take a floor for the same reason, `Array[union(<receiver elements>)]`: its fall-through is not merely wider, because `BlockFolding` folds it on a `Constant` block, so `seen = 0; [1, 2].select do |e| seen += 1; seen == 2 end` answered a provably-empty `[]` where Ruby answers `[2]`. A `Constant<Range>` receiver's elements MUST be widened through `widen_value_pinned` first: they are values the walk enumerated, the RBS answer the floor replaces carried no pin, and a pinned `Array[1 | 2 | 3 | 4]` refuses the widening a later `<<` needs, so `q = (1..4).filter { … }; q << 9; q.last == 9` would fold always-falsey. A Tuple receiver's pins are the literal's own and stay. No other method in the fold's set takes a floor: `map`'s assembler cannot decline, and `BlockFolding` never folds `filter_map` / `flat_map`, so their fall-through is the RBS `Array[U]` projection.
  - **Above the per-element threading arity cap, and under nesting suppression.** A position is typed tail-only above the cap, and at ANY arity when the fold runs nested inside a block body another pass is evaluating whole — the scope-threading fold, the `next` join, the break-arm collection, the #587 (b) fixpoint, or an enclosing per-element walk that is itself above the cap — each of which suppresses the threading so that it never re-enters. For a tail that observes a name the body's prefix binds or mutates in place, tail-only is the ENTRY binding the prefix has already falsified. In both cases the fold MUST floor every position to `Dynamic[top]` when the tail depends on such a name that neither #587 (b) binding answers — a mutated block PARAMETER (`([[]] * 9).map do |a| a << 1; a end` answered nine provably-empty `[]`, and `[[], []].map do |a| a << w; a end` inside a threaded `m.synchronize do w = v; … end` answered two), or a captured local or instance variable whose in-place widening declined (`ks = ENV.keys; [1, …, 9].map do |e| ks << e; ks.last end` reads the precise `Array[String]` entry binding, so counted as answered it would type nine `String`s for the Integers it appends). Names the rebind fixpoint covers, rebound locals and ivars alike (compared with their sigil), and captured locals and ivars the in-place widening BINDS MUST NOT be floored: neither binding's cost depends on the arity (`out = []; [1, …, 9].map do |e| out << e; out.size end` reads nine `non-negative-int`), the in-place widening evaluates no body and so holds under the suppression too, and there a rebound name already takes the escaping-block floor name by name. Two dependencies MUST NOT be floored either: a name with no ENTRY binding (a body-local such as `key = k.to_s; [key, n + w]`), which tail-only already reads as `Dynamic[top]`, so the floor would only erase the structure around it — instance and global variables always count as bound; and any dependency of a body with a block-level `next`, which the `next` join evaluates whole whatever the suppression says. The floor replaces each position's block result, so the assembler still decides the call: `map` answers a `Tuple` of `Dynamic[top]`, `flat_map` declines to its RBS projection, and the find and filter families take their floors above. The names MUST come from the scan that gates the threading itself, so "would threading have changed this tail" and "is tail-only untrustworthy here" stay one question, and a body that scan answers false for — a single-statement body, or one whose tail ignores its prefix — keeps its exact fold.
  - **The generic block-return pass under nesting suppression.** The dispatcher's block-return pass (`ExpressionTyper#type_block_body`, reached through `block_return_for` for every block-bearing call no fold answers, and through the `inject` / `reduce` fold's block typing) is typed tail-only under the same suppression, and there a tail that observes a name its prefix binds or mutates in place MUST NOT read that name's entry binding. It MUST type the tail over the entry scope with each such name — the same scan's names — answered without evaluating the prefix: a name the prefix rebinds reads `Dynamic[top]`, and a name it only mutates in place reads `Inference::UnknownStoreWidening.widen` of its entry binding over the prefix's mutation sites, the binding the #587 (b) in-place widening lays for a capture. The answer is per name, not per block, so the structure around the name survives. It MUST be limited to the names tail-only answers stale, so these keep their entry binding: a name the entry scope does not bind, which already reads `Dynamic[top]`; an instance variable on its ADR-58 declaration-sourced binding that the prefix only rebinds, since that seed is the union of every write in the class — its in-place mutation sites MUST still widen that seed, rebound or not, because no write shows a mutation (`@out << w.to_s; @out` over a `+"k"` seed read `"k"` and fired always-truthy on `r == "k"`); a name the #587 (b) binding laid under the pass answers; and a name whose widening declines — a nominal `String` under `<<`, a precise nominal `Array[String]` — since the threaded body reads the same binding. What this reaches is every name no #587 (b) binding answers: a block parameter, and a capture of a block the call runs at most once, of an iterator that discards its block's value or that the catalogue does not know, or of the `inject` fold, and an instance variable on its class-wide seed, which the #587 (b) binding leaves out. `{ x: [], y: [] }.map { |_k, a| a << w; a }` inside a threaded `m.synchronize do w = v; … end` therefore types `Array[Array[Dynamic[top]]]`, never `Array[[]]`; `i = 0; … m.synchronize { i += w; i }` there types `Dynamic[top]`, never `0`, so a later `k == 0` no longer folds always-truthy; `xs.map { |e| e = e.to_s; [e, w] }` types `Array[[Dynamic[top], 1]]`; and `s = String.new; … m.synchronize { s << "x"; s }` still types `String`. A `rescue` body and a prefix that can `break` stay tail-only with or without the suppression, as the threading gate leaves them.
  - **`Constant[String]` under a String mutator.** `MutationWidening.widen_for_mutator` MUST widen a String-valued `Constant` to the bare `String` nominal for every receiver-mutating String method: every bang method `String` defines, and the non-bang mutators `<<`, `concat`, `insert`, `prepend`, `replace`, `clear`, `[]=`, `setbyte`, `bytesplice`, `append_as_bytes` and `force_encoding`. That list is `Inference::StringMutation::MUTATORS`, and it MUST be the only String mutator table: `MutationWidening::SHAPE_MUTATORS` MUST be derived from it alongside the Array and Hash ones so the block-return threading scan and the widening it predicts cannot drift apart, and `Effects::MutationClassifier` and the effect catalogue's `mutators: string` MUST read it rather than keep a list of their own — a second list had drifted and missed `force_encoding`, and a third, `ContentJoin`'s String adders, named only names the Array adders already carried. The class-level ivar census (ADR-58), the constant and class-variable census and the `IndexedNarrowing` invalidation MUST ask `SHAPE_MUTATORS` rather than the Array and Hash tables alone, and the ivar census MUST widen a String-valued `Constant` member of an ivar's seed to `String` once any method in the class calls a String mutator on the ivar. `StatementEvaluator`'s content scans — the joins the loop, block-capture and `each_with_object` seams run, and the ADR-56 callee and escaping-closure floors — MUST count every String mutator no Array or Hash table lists alongside the content adders (`StatementEvaluator::CONTENT_MUTATORS`): a String carries no element parameter, so a join answers a String pre-state with `String` and a floor floors it, and without them `def strip(s) = s.delete_prefix!("a")` or an escaping `-> { s.upcase! }` left the caller's literal pinned. Since the scans cannot see the receiver's class, a pre-state that is a union with a String member (`Array | String`, `Hash | String`, `String?`) MUST be joined and floored member by member — the String members to `String`, the rest as the collection they are, and a member no mutation can fill (`nil`) left as it is — and the Array join MUST read only an Array adder's arguments as elements, the Hash join only `[]=` / `store`'s as pairs. Joined whole, `x.force_encoding(e)` on an `[1] | "ab"` capture typed it `"ab" | Array[1 | Encoding]` and an escaping closure's floor typed it `Array[untyped]`, so `case x when String` reported an unreachable clause. A floor rebinds the same object rather than writing a new one, so it MUST keep the local's ADR-58 declaration-sourced mark and its issue #286 optimistic nil-freeness mark (`StatementEvaluator#with_floored_local`): floored through a plain rebind, `r = @name; -> { r << "!" }.call; r.size` over a declaration-seeded `@name` reported a nil receiver on correct code. A name an Array or Hash table also lists (`clear`, `slice!`, `reverse!`) is left to those tables' adders there, since the Array join would otherwise have to tell a remover from an adder it does not know. The callee floor floors a refined String to `String` whatever the mutator, so a callee that only calls `upcase!` still drops a caller's `non-empty-string`: the scans record where a mutation happens, not which. No String mutator may be safe to constant-fold (`Builtins::STRING_CATALOG`). Only the pinned VALUE is at stake, so there is no `values: :keep` half to preserve. Non-mutating siblings (`upcase` vs `upcase!`, `delete_prefix` vs `delete_prefix!`) MUST stay precise. A `non-empty-string` `Difference` MUST keep its witness only under a mutator call that cannot leave a non-empty receiver empty (`StringMutation.may_empty?`): the appenders (`<<`, `concat`, `insert`, `prepend`, `append_as_bytes`) and the rewrites that keep at least one character (`setbyte`, `force_encoding`, the case mappings, `reverse!`, `succ!` / `next!`, `squeeze!`, `unicode_normalize!`), listed as `StringMutation::EMPTY_PRESERVING`, and `tr!` / `tr_s!` when their second argument is provably a non-empty String, since they map every matched character to one character of the replacement. Every other mutator call MUST retract it, so a mutator nobody classified costs precision rather than soundness: `"a".tr!("a", "")` is `""`, as are `delete_prefix!`, `delete_suffix!`, `bytesplice`, and `encode!` / `scrub!` with an empty replacement, on the right input. A spec holds the table to every bang method `String` defines, to exactly the public methods that raise `FrozenError` on a frozen receiver, and to every method the builtin catalogue tags `c_effects: mutate`. `s = +"ab"; s << "c"` therefore types `s` as `String`, so `s == "ab"` no longer folds always-truthy on correct code — the ADR-56 block-capture path already widened the same binding, and the two seams MUST NOT disagree about one carrier. `s = +"ab"; s.delete_prefix!("a")` types `s` as `String` too, and an ivar seeded `@s = +"ab"` reads `String` in every method of a class that calls `@s << "c"` anywhere.
- The index sibling of issue #617's compound-write rule. `ExpressionTyper` MUST type `h[k] op= v` / `h[k] ||= v` / `h[k] &&= v` as the value it stores through `[]=` — the issue #1140 stored value above: the `[]` read, refined by a recorded indexed narrowing, combined with `v` by the same algebra — never as `v` alone, with the one exception below. It MUST read that value from `StatementEvaluator#index_compound_write_value` rather than re-derive it; the widening and the narrowing record are scope effects and stay with the straight-line `eval_index_or_write` / `eval_index_write` handlers. Both paths MUST perform the `[]` read through the tiers a plain `h[k]` call takes, in its order (`ExpressionTyper#implicit_index_read_type`): the own-`def` override check, the dispatcher's node- and scope-gated tiers, then the project-`def` inference a dispatch miss falls to. No `[]` call node exists in the tree, so the write node stands in as the call context; it carries the `receiver` / `arguments` / `block` those tiers read, and the dispatcher's records land on it as they would on a call — so a `[]` read the core RBS types past its `%a{implicitly-returns-nil}` marks the write optimistic, and `if (xs[i] &&= "y")` keeps its else arm, since an absent slot stores nothing and the write is `nil`. A read dispatched on the receiver's type alone skipped those tiers, so a project `[]` with no signature — `class C; def [](k) = 0; def []=(k, v); end; end; c[:a] += 1` — typed the write `Dynamic[top]` while the plain `c[:a]` typed `0`; it now types `1`. Under a splat index (`c[*keys] += v`) the body tiers MUST decline: the dispatcher alone answers, or `Dynamic[top]` when an own `def` overrides an inherited project declaration. The splat's expansion decides the arity, and binding the one untyped argument that stands in for it folded `def [](*keys) = keys.size` to `1`. Two tiers stay out of reach. A plugin `dynamic_return` rule written for `[]` does not answer the read: its `methods:` gate matches `call_node.name`, which an index write lacks, and the shipped rules decline any node that is not a `Prism::CallNode`, by an explicit check or through the rescue around a rule that reads `name` anyway. Minting one would put a node that is not in the tree in front of plugins, through a constructor whose field list changed within the accepted `prism` range. The nil-stripped retry a plain call makes on a `T | nil` receiver does not run either, so `h[k] += v` on an optional receiver still reads `Dynamic[top]`. The exception is the memoization idiom, the variable rule's optimism keyed on the slot rather than the variable: a `||=` whose `[]` read is a lone `Dynamic` MUST read as `v` when `v` has a truthy part. With no evidence about the slot, the ADR-5 optimistic reading is that the value the idiom returns is the one it stores. Its known cost is a slot something the analyzer did not see has set, such as a caller's argument (`def m(opts = {}) = opts[:mode] ||= :fast`, the issue #544 shape). `Dynamic[top] | v` sent every memoizing method — `@local_targets[name] ||= new(...)` on an ivar the method never writes, `(@extended ||= {})[[labels, base.labels]] ||= build` whose non-literal key the empty shape cannot answer, `CACHE[key] ||= build(key)` — to `sig.skipped.untyped-return`, where the rvalue reading had typed it. Only there does the value position depart from the statement handler, which keeps `Dynamic[top] | v`. The exception MUST NOT extend further: a read that is a union with a `Dynamic` member (`Dynamic[top]?`) has evidence and takes the stored value — including one a project `[]` body infers (`return nil unless @data; @data[k]`, `super || 0`), so a memoizing method over such a `[]` is `sig.skipped.untyped-return` rather than typed as its rvalue; `&&=` is no memo (`h[k] &&= v` on an absent slot is `nil`); an rvalue with no truthy part stores nothing truthy, so the slot is the answer whenever it is set — a `bot` rvalue is a guard (`opts[k] ||= raise KeyError`), and `@flags[n] ||= false` is `true` once another method stored `true`; and an operator write has no optimistic reading, as an unbound operator-write variable target reads `Dynamic[top]`. Nor does it apply at a site a block-return pass marks as repeated (the next entry). Typed as `v`, a block tail pinned every position of the fold to the increment: `counts = Hash.new(0); r = %w[a b a].map { |w| counts[w] += 1 }` typed `[1, 1, 1]` and fired a `flow.always-truthy-condition` on `r.last == 1`, which is `2` at runtime; it now types `[Integer, Integer, Integer]`.
- The index `||=` sites of a repeating block body. A block-return pass types every run of a repeating body from one entry scope, so under it a slot that reads a lone `Dynamic` may hold what an EARLIER run stored, and the memoization exception above MUST NOT read it as having no evidence. The per-element fold (the Tuple / Range fold and the per-pair `HashShape` fold, § issue #587 (b)) and the generic block-return pass MUST mark the sites `Inference::RepeatedOrWrites.sites` picks in the scope they type the body under (`Scope#with_repeated_or_writes`, laid with the #587 (b) binding), and `StatementEvaluator#index_compound_write_value` MUST NOT take the exception at a marked site (`Scope#repeated_or_write?`). The mark MUST be keyed on the site, the `IndexOrWriteNode` by identity, and not on its receiver: nothing in a receiver's type says that its slot was filled — `{}` widens to `Hash[Dynamic[top], Dynamic[top]]`, a bare `Hash` is not widened at all, and a constant, a class variable, an ADR-58-seeded instance variable, an attribute reader or a nested memo (`(cache[:a] ||= {})[:b] ||= e`) is no binding the pass rebinds — and a mark on a binding is lost to the narrowing that rebinds it (`c && (c[:first] ||= e) == 2`). No transition drops the mark, and a join keeps a site either arm marks, since the mark only withholds the exception. The `inject` / `reduce` constant fold lays no #587 (b) binding at all and marks nothing ([#1332](https://github.com/rigortype/rigor/issues/1332)), and neither does the ADR-56 captured-local write-back, which types the body through `StatementEvaluator`.

  The sites are the `IndexOrWriteNode`s of the body, at any depth short of a nested `def` body, less those whose receiver is fresh at every run — a hash or array literal, `.new` called on a constant, or a local only the body binds (not captured, not a block parameter or block-local) whose every write stores such a fresh object, read at its own slots — so `(Hash.new[:first] ||= e) == 2` and `c = Hash.new; (c[:first] ||= e) == 2` keep their exact folds. A fresh local's deeper slots are not fresh: `box = [shared]; (box[0][:first] ||= e) == 2` reads the `shared` an earlier position filled. Of those:
